Preface
Function spaces and their applications to the boundedness of operators have always
been one of the core topics in harmonic analysis, with Lebesgue spaces Lp(Rn) for p ∈
(0, ∞] serving as one of the most fundamental and widely used examples. As a refine-
ment of the Lebesgue scale, the Lorentz spaces Lp,q(Rn) with q ∈(0, ∞] were introduced
and studied by Lorentz in the early 1950s [157, 158]. Lorentz spaces naturally contain
Lebesgue spaces and weak-Lebesgue spaces as special cases, with
Lp,p(Rn) = Lp(Rn) and Lp,∞(Rn) = weak −Lp(Rn),
and they also arise as intermediate spaces between Lp(Rn) and L∞(Rn) via the real inter-
polation method (see, for example, [38, 143]). It is well known that, due to their finer
structural properties, Lorentz spaces play an important role in addressing various critical
or endpoint problems across different areas of analysis, including harmonic analysis (see,
for example, [171, 180, 194, 204]) and partial differential equations (see, for example,
[120, 163, 184]). For further studies on Lorentz spaces defined over Rn, such as their
dual spaces, we refer to the works of Hunt [118], Cwikel [56], Cwikel and Fefferman
[57, 58], as well as [18, 67, 128, 164, 188, 199]. This paradigm of refining classical
scales extends naturally to the theory of Hardy spaces, which also plays an important role
in harmonic analysis (see, for example, [43, 47, 50, 71, 79, 132, 133, 136, 154, 226])
and partial differential equations and has been extensively studied (see, for example,
[32, 33, 48, 87, 169, 191, 195, 200]). Recall that the classical Hardy space Hp(Rn) with
p ∈(0, 1] was originally introduced by Stein and Weiss [198] and further studied by
Fefferman and Stein [71]. In particular, when p ∈(0, 1], in studying the boundedness
of certain classes of singular integral operators, Hp(Rn) serves as a good substitute for
Lp(Rn), where, in the endpoint case, Hp(Rn) is often replaced by the weak Hardy space
Hp,∞(Rn). Indeed, Alvarez and Milman [13] proved that a convolutional δ-type Calder´on–
Zygmund operator T with δ ∈(0, 1] and T ∗1 = 0, where T ∗denotes the adjoint operator
of T on L2(Rn), is bounded on Hp(Rn) for any given p ∈( n
n+δ, 1]. However, in the end-
point case p =
n
n+δ, T may fail to be bounded on H
n
n+δ(Rn). Liu [144] later showed that in
this case T is bounded from H
n
n+δ(Rn) to H
n
n+δ,∞(Rn). Moreover, in order to find the largest
function space from which the Hilbert transform is bounded into the weak Lebesgue space
L1,∞(Rn), Fefferman and Soria [70] introduced the weak Hardy space H1,∞(Rn) and, using
its ∞-atomic characterization, proved that a convolutional Calder´on–Zygmund operator
whose kernel satisfies the Dini-type condition is bounded from H1,∞(Rn) to L1,∞(Rn). For
more investigations on Hardy spaces and their variants, we refer to the well-known mono-
graphs [65, 73, 80, 87, 159, 197, 200, 205] and the articles by Bonami et al. [20, 21], Bui
et al. [35, 34, 36, 37], Cruz-Uribe et al. [51, 52, 53, 54, 55], Grafakos et al. [92, 93], Ho
[110, 112, 114], Nakai and Sawano [176, 177], and Sawano et al. [189, 191]. We also

refer to [11, 12, 63, 64, 113, 142, 201, 202, 220] for further results on weak Hardy spaces
and to [23, 30, 62, 64, 85, 95, 150, 175, 233] for the boundedness of operators on (weak)
Hardy spaces.
In 1974, Fefferman et al. [69] introduced the classical Hardy–Lorentz spaces Hp,q(Rn),
with p ∈(0, ∞) and q ∈(0, ∞], which include the Hardy space Hp(Rn) and the weak
Hardy space Hp,∞(Rn) as special cases and which also naturally appear as the intermediate
spaces of Hp(Rn) and L∞(Rn) under real interpolation. Parilov [182] later obtained atomic
characterizations of H1,q(Rn) for q ∈(1, ∞), and in 2007 Abu-Shammala and Torchinsky
[1] investigated Hp,q(Rn) with p ∈(0, 1] and q ∈(0, ∞). In particular, they established
their ∞-atomic characterizations, real interpolation properties in the parameter q, and
boundedness results for Calder´on–Zygmund and related operators on these spaces. Since
then, various extensions of the classical Hardy–Lorentz spaces have been well developed
in different settings. In particular, Almeida and Caetano [3] introduced generalized Hardy
spaces which include the spaces Hp,q(Rn) from [1] as special cases, and obtained their
maximal characterizations, real interpolation results with the function parameters, and
boundedness results for certain classical operators acting on these spaces. More recently,
Jiao et al. [124] introduced the variable Hardy–Lorentz spaces Hp(·),q(Rn), where p(·) is
a nonnegative Lebesgue measurable function on Rn and q ∈(0, ∞]. They established
their various real-variable characterizations involving atoms and Littlewood–Paley func-
tions, and further obtained results concerning real interpolation, duality, and boundedness
properties of some singular integral operators on these spaces. In the anisotropic set-
ting, Liu et al. [147, 149] introduced the anisotropic Hardy–Lorentz spaces Hp,q
A (Rn) with
p ∈(0, 1] and q ∈(0, ∞], where A is a general expansive matrix on Rn. They established
several real-variable characterizations, respectively, in terms of (finite) atoms, molecules,
maximal functions, and Littlewood–Paley functions, and applied these to characterize the
dual space of Hp,q
A (Rn) and obtain the boundedness of Calder´on–Zygmund operators from
Hp,q
A (Rn) to Lp,q(Rn), including the corresponding critical case. Very recently, Liu et al.
[146, 148] extended the results in [147, 149] to anisotropic variable Hardy–Lorentz spaces
Hp(·),q
A
(Rn), which include the variable exponent spaces Hp(·),q(Rn) from [124] as special
cases. Independently, Almeida et al. [2] investigated the anisotropic variable Hardy–
Lorentz spaces Hp(·),q(·)(Rn, A), where p(·) and q(·) are nonnegative Lebesgue measurable
functions on (0, ∞) and A is a general expansive matrix on Rn. In [2], they obtained
equivalent characterizations of Hp(·),q(·)(Rn, A) using maximal functions and atoms. No-
tably, the two anisotropic variable Hardy–Lorentz space theories in [2] and [146, 148] are
not directly comparable, since in the former the variable exponents p(·) are only defined
on (0, ∞) rather than on Rn. It is worth noting that anisotropic Euclidean spaces serve
as concrete and important models of spaces of homogeneous type studied in this mono-
graph. For research conducted in the anisotropic Euclidean setting, we refer to the articles
by Bownik et al. [22, 24, 25, 26, 27, 28, 29, 134], Cleanthous et al. [43, 44, 45], and Ho
[109]. In particular, a differential characterization for anisotropic Hardy spaces using a
parabolic differential equation of Calder´on and Torchinsky has been established in [29].
For further results on Hardy–Lorentz spaces on Rn, we refer to the articles by Jiao et al.
[123, 215, 236] and Weisz et al. [101, 121, 122, 214] and also to more related articles
[111, 140, 141, 145, 155, 156, 210, 212].
One fundamental development in function space theory came with the consideration of
function spaces defined on environments much more general than the Euclidean ambient.
In particular, in the 1970s, Coifman and Weiss [49, 50] introduced the notion of a space of


## Page 5


Preface
iii
homogeneous type, which is a triplet (X, ρ, µ), where (X, ρ) is a quasi-metric space and µ
is a doubling measure, as a natural setting for the study of function spaces and the bound-
edness of operators arising in harmonic analysis. In this context, Coifman and Weiss [50]
introduced the atomic Hardy space Hp,q
CW(X) with p ∈(0, 1] and q ∈(p, ∞]∩[1, ∞], proved
that Hp,q
CW(X) is independent of the choice of q (and therefore can be denoted simply by
Hp
CW(X)), and further showed that the dual of Hp
CW(X) is the Lipschitz space L 1
p −1(X) when
p ∈(0, 1), or the John–Nirenberg class of functions of bounded mean oscillation BMO (X)
when p = 1. Given the natural limitations of such a general setting, the spaces Hp
CW(X) are
only expected to coincide with their Euclidean counterparts when p is sufficiently close to
1, since higher-order vanishing moments involving polynomials are typically unavailable
on X. Nevertheless, a rich theory of Hardy spaces has since developed. Indeed, spaces
of homogeneous type have become the most natural and general underlying spaces for
developing the real-variable theory of various function spaces and their applications to
the boundedness of operators. We refer to the monographs [7, 61, 107, 168, 190], the
articles by Alvarado et al. [4, 5, 8, 9, 10], Georgiadis et al. [81, 82, 83, 84], Nakai et al.
[127, 173, 174, 178, 196, 203], and Sawano et al. [192, 193], and more related articles
[129, 142, 160, 207, 219] for further studies about function spaces and their applications
to the boundedness of operators on spaces of homogeneous type.
In [50], Coifman and Weiss also established the radial maximal function characteriza-
tion of H1
CW(X), provided that an additional geometric assumption is imposed on the space
(X, ρ, µ). This led them to ask what extent additional geometric conditions are necessary
for the validity of such characterizations. This question has since inspired a substantial
body of work aimed at establishing various real-variable characterizations of the atomic
Hardy spaces on X under minimal assumptions on the underlying ambient space. Early
progress in this vein was made in certain subclasses of spaces of homogeneous type.
More specifically, although the measure of any dilated ball λB with λ ∈[1, ∞) has a
quantitative upper bound estimate in a space of homogeneous type, it was recognized that
there are situations where a more precise lower bound estimate of the measure of λB is
needed, beyond merely comparing the relative sizes of λB and B. With the Euclidean
space Rn as the canonical example, this need naturally led to the study of n-Ahlfors-
regular spaces, which are spaces of homogeneous type where the measure of a ball is
required to be exactly comparable to its radius raised to the power n. In the setting of
1-Ahlfors-regular spaces, Mac´ıas and Segovia [161] characterized Hp
CW(X) via the grand
maximal function when p ∈(0, 1] is sufficiently close to 1, Li [135] obtained a new max-
imal function characterization of Hp
CW(X) using the space of test functions introduced in
[100], and Duong and Yan [66] characterized Hardy spaces via the Lusin area function
associated with certain semigroup. Since then, Ahlfors-regular spaces have continued to
attract considerable attention in the study of the real-variable theory of function spaces;
see, for example, [7, 66, 135, 160, 161]. Further insight into this question came from
realizing that a robust function space theory can still be developed in settings more gen-
eral than Ahlfors-regular spaces. This led to the study of RD-spaces, which are spaces of
homogeneous type where the equipped measure additionally satisfies a reverse doubling
condition. Such spaces provide a natural framework for studying the real-variable theory
of function spaces and the boundedness of operators. For further studies about function
spaces and their applications to the boundedness of operators on RD-spaces, we refer to
[89, 90, 98, 99, 130, 131, 227, 228, 229, 230].
Beyond geometric considerations, analytic tools such as Calder´on reproducing for-


## Page 6


iv
Preface
mulae play an equally fundamental role in function space theory, providing a powerful
tool for establishing characterizations of function spaces and proving boundedness re-
sults for operators. Recall that the classical Calder´on reproducing formulae on Rn gain
their prototypes from Calder´on [38], in which problems of the intermediate space and the
interpolation were studied via a reproducing method. In 1974, Heideman [107] gave a
detailed treatment of the homogeneous and the inhomogeneous continuous Calder´on re-
producing formulae and used them to study duality and fractional integrals on Lipschitz
spaces (see also [39, 40]). Since then, Calder´on reproducing formulae and their variants
have become indispensable in the study of a wide range of function spaces, including
Hardy spaces (see, for example, [34, 36, 89, 229]), Besov spaces and Triebel–Lizorkin
spaces (see, for example, [74, 75, 76, 183, 221, 222, 230]), and also more general scale of
function spaces (see, for example, [224, 225, 232]). These tools also have further appli-
cations in proving the boundedness of Calder´on–Zygmund operators (see, for example,
[151, 152, 153]). For more applications of Calder´on reproducing formulae, particularly
in the study of Musielak–Orlicz Hardy spaces and variable exponent Hardy spaces, we
refer to [68, 105, 137, 176, 220, 223, 235].
Despite their success in the Euclidean setting, Calder´on reproducing formulae were
initially unavailable in the setting of a space of homogeneous type X, leading much of the
work on function spaces and their applications to the operator boundedness in this context,
including [66, 135, 161] mentioned above, to rely on additional geometrical assumptions
on the underlying space, such as the reverse doubling property of the measure (see, for
example, [89, 90, 193, 235]). Recently, a breakthrough on the analysis over X without any
additional geometrical assumptions came with the work of Auscher and Hyt¨onen [16, 17],
who, using the system of random dyadic cubes established by Hyt¨onen and Kairema
[119], constructed an orthonormal wavelet basis of L2(X) with exponential decay. Later,
Han et al. [97] established the wavelet reproducing formulae, which hold in both the
sense of spaces of test functions and distributions. Motivated by these advances, He et al.
[103] introduced an approximation of the identity with exponential decay and obtained
new Calder´on reproducing formulae on X. Together, these advances overcame initial
obstacles in function space theory and laid the foundation for developing the real-variable
theory of function spaces and for studying the operator boundedness in general spaces of
homogeneous type.
Very recently, He et al. [102, 106] completely answered the aforementioned ques-
tion of Coifman and Weiss by developing a real-variable theory of Hardy spaces and
their localized versions, including various equivalent characterizations and the bound-
edness of sublinear operators, valid for p ∈( n
n+θ, 1], on spaces of homogeneous type
with upper dimension n ∈(0, ∞), where θ ∈(0, 1) is the H¨older continuity exponent
of the orthonormal wavelet basis from [16].
Fu et al.
[77] and Zhou et al.
[234]
further generalized the corresponding results in [102], respectively, to Musielak–Orlicz
Hardy spaces and Hardy–Lorentz spaces. Moreover, Yan et al. [217, 218] introduced
the Hardy-type spaces HY(X), associated with ball quasi-Banach function spaces Y(X)
on X, and developed a complete real-variable theory of these spaces. We also refer to
[8, 78, 94, 96, 104, 201, 202, 207, 209, 216] for further related studies.
One issue that arises in the consideration of Hardy spaces Hp(X) in the setting of a
space of homogeneous type X is that unless p ∈(0, 1] is sufficiently near to 1, Hp(X)
spaces become trivial, in the sense that Hp(X) only contains constant elements. At the
heart of the matter is the fact that spaces of H¨older continuous functions reduce to ex-

v
actly constant functions if the smoothness parameter is too large. In the Euclidean set-
ting, for example, H¨older spaces of any order α > 1 are trivial; however, this upper
smoothness bound need not be 1 in an arbitrary quasi-metric space. Therefore, a cen-
tral question—one that implicitly underpins the work of Coifman and Weiss [50]—is to
determine the range of p, depending on the geometry of X, for which a satisfactory the-
ory of Hardy spaces exists. Coifman and Weiss [50] mentioned an unspecified range of
p for which their construction works. The first significant attempt to clarify this range
of p in a quantitative fashion is due to Mac´ıas and Segovia [161], who, in the setting
of 1-Ahlfors-regular spaces, obtained a grand maximal function characterization for the
Coifman–Weiss atomic Hardy spaces Hp
CW(X) for p ∈(pρ, 1], where

spaces on spaces of homogeneous type and having been widely cited (see, for example,

consequence of working within such a general framework. An important advance toward
addressing these limitations was provided by Mitrea et al. [168] who introduced and
studied the index

In order to set the stage for the main results in this monograph, let us briefly recall the
idea of the construction of the approximation of the identity on spaces of homogeneous
type (X, ρ, µ), which is originally due to Coifman; see also [59, p. 40]. More precisely,
choose a smooth function h : [0, ∞) →[0, ∞) such that h(x) = 1 for any x ∈[0, 1

In this monograph, we begin by constructing a maximally smooth approximation of
the identity of order 0 < ε ⪯ind (X, ρ) on general quasi-ultrametric spaces of homoge-
neous type (X, ρ, µ), which we then use to prove the boundedness of Calder´on–Zygmund
operators on spaces of test functions and thereby establish sharp Calder´on reproducing
formulae on ultra-RD-spaces (see Definition 1.1.24). As applications, we also obtain the
Littlewood–Paley function characterizations of Hardy spaces and Triebel–Lizorkin spaces
on ultra-RD-spaces. Finally, we establish a complete real-variable theory including equiv-
alent characterizations and boundedness of operators on Hardy–Lorentz spaces Hp,q(X) in
quasi-ultrametric spaces of homogeneous type. It is remarkable that, in this monograph,
we only assume that µ is Borel-semiregular, that singletons may have strictly positive
measure, and that ρ is a quasi-ultrametric, which provides a more general theoretic set-
ting than the space of homogeneous type in the sense of Coifman and Weiss. Moreover,
we point out that the maximal order of approximation of the identity and the regularity
exponent of Calder´on reproducing formulae obtained in this monograph are sharp, since
they are based on the sharp regularity of the quasi-ultrametric. Given the sharpness of
the tools constructed in this monograph, we are able to develop the real-variable theory
of Hp,q(X) for sharp ranges of p and q, under minimal assumptions on the space X. In
addition, all results presented in this monograph are new and appear in print for the first
time.
To be precise, the whole monograph consists of five chapters.
As a foundational step, in Chapter 1, we construct a maximally smooth approximation
of the identity on quasi-ultrametric spaces of homogeneous type. We begin by laying
out several necessary conventions regarding notation and recalling the basic notions of
the underlying spaces. We then introduce a new type of approximation of the identity
with bounded support on quasi-ultrametric spaces of homogeneous type. By an argument
similar to that used in the proof of [7, Theorem 3.22] and the properties of the regularized
quasi-ultrametric, we prove the existence of such approximation of the identity, which
is the main result of this chapter and extends [7, Theorem 3.22] from Ahlfors-regular
quasi-ultrametric spaces to quasi-ultrametric spaces of homogeneous type.
Building on this foundation, Chapter 2 establishes sharp Calder´on reproducing formu-
lae on ultra-RD-spaces X. Specifically, we first recall the notions of test functions and
distributions on X. By successfully constructing maximally smooth cut-off functions, we
prove the boundedness on spaces of test functions of Calder´on–Zygmund operators whose
kernels satisfy certain size and regularity conditions. To obtain sharp homogeneous con-
tinuous Calder´on reproducing formulae, we first divide the identity operator into a main
operator TN and a remainder operator RN for any fixed N ∈N. Then, by using some
properties of the composition of two homogeneous approximations of the identity, we
prove that the operator norms of RN on both L2(X) and spaces of test functions can be
made sufficiently small if N is sufficiently large, which ensures that T −1
N
exists and is
bounded on both L2(X) and spaces of test functions. From this and the homogeneous
approximation of the identity, we establish sharp homogeneous continuous Calder´on re-
producing formulae on X. Moreover, by an argument similar to that used in the proof of
the homogeneous case and by the inhomogeneous approximation of the identity, we also

other hand, to establish sharp homogeneous discrete Calder´on reproducing formulae, we
first split the k-level dyadic cubes into a sum of dyadic subcubes in level k + j and then
divide the identity operator into the Riemann sum operator SN and the remainder operator
RN. By proving that the operator norms of RN on both L2(X) and spaces of test functions
can be made sufficiently small if both N and j are sufficiently large, we conclude that S−1
N
exists and is bounded on both L2(X) and spaces of test functions, which, combined with
the homogeneous approximation of the identity, further implies sharp homogeneous dis-
crete Calder´on reproducing formulae on X. We also obtain sharp inhomogeneous discrete
Calder´on reproducing formulae X by a similar argument. All of these sharp Calder´on
reproducing formulae are established in the sense of spaces of test functions, distribu-
tions, and Lp(X) with p ∈(1, ∞). The range of parameters for the spaces of test functions
and distributions in which Calder´on reproducing formulae hold is sharp, given the sharp
regularity of both Calder´on–Zygmund operators and approximations of the identity.
As a first application, Chapter 3 characterizes the Lusin-area-function-based Hardy
space on ultra-RD-spaces (X, ρ, µ) via atoms and various Littlewood–Paley functions. We
begin by defining the Hardy space Hp(X) via the Lusin area function and prove, using
the sharp homogeneous discrete Calder´on reproducing formula and the Fefferman–Stein
vector-valued maximal inequality on X, that this definition of Hp(X) is independent of the
choice of the approximation of the identity. By utilizing the sharp homogeneous continu-
ous Calder´on reproducing formula, we obtain the atomic characterization of Hp(X). From
this and a key lemma concerning the Littlewood–Paley auxiliary function, we further de-
duce the Littlewood–Paley g-function and the g∗
λ-function characterizations of Hp(X) for
the best-known range of λ. In particular, in the special case when X is an n-Ahlfors-regular
space as in [7], the main results in this chapter give brand new characterizations of the
Hardy space studied in [7] via the Lusin area function, the Littlewood–Paley g-function,
and the Littlewood–Paley g∗

A salient feature of the work undertaken in Chapter 5 is the range of p in (0.0.8), which
captures, in an optimal and quantitative manner, the relationship between the geometry

## Page 17


Chapter 1
A Maximally Smooth Approximation
of the Identity on Quasi-Ultrametric
Spaces of Homogeneous Type
The main target of this chapter is to establish an approximation of the identity on quasi-
ultrametric spaces of homogeneous type that exhibits a maximal degree of smoothness
(measured on the H¨older scale) that the underlying ambient space can permit. To this end,
in Section 1.1, we first recall some definitions and basic properties of quasi-ultrametric
and quasi-ultrametric spaces. Then we present the notion of quasi-ultrametric measure
spaces, which includes spaces such as quasi-ultrametric spaces of homogeneous type,
ultra-RD-spaces, and Ahlfors-regular quasi-ultrametric spaces, and list some basic as-
sumptions on the underlying quasi-ultrametric measure space. Next, we recall the lower
smoothness index of quasi-ultrametric spaces, which plays an important role through-
out this monograph. In Section 1.2, we introduce a maximally smooth approximation of
the identity with bounded support. Then we prove its existence and further obtain some
useful results related to this approximation of the identity on quasi-ultrametric spaces of
homogeneous type.
1.1
Underlying Quasi-Ultrametric Measure Spaces
In this section, we recall some definitions and basic properties of quasi-ultrametric and
quasi-ultrametric spaces, and then present the notion of quasi-ultrametric measure spaces
and some basic assumptions that we will use throughout this monograph. To this end,
we first begin with recalling the definition of quasi-ultrametrics; see, for instance, [7,
pp. 34–35].
Definition 1.1.1. Let X be a nonempty set. A function ρ : X × X →R+ is called a
quasi-ultrametric if there exist two constants C0,C1 ∈(0, ∞) such that, for any x, y, z ∈X,
(i) ρ(x, y) = 0 if and only if x = y;
(ii) (the quasi-symmetry condition) ρ(y, x) ≤C0ρ(x, y);
(iii) (the quasi-ultrametric condition) ρ(x, y) ≤C1 max{ρ(x, z), ρ(z, y)}.

An approximation of the identity on quasi-ultrametric spaces of homogeneous type
Now, we recall the definition of the lower smoothness index of a quasi-ultrametric
space, which was originally introduced by Mitrea et al. in [168, Definition 4.26]. This
index plays a pivotal role in identifying the optimal range of p for which there exists a rich
theory of Hardy spaces Hp(X) (see, for example, [7]). More specifically, the index permits
us to determine just how far p can be below 1 while still having a maximal function
characterization of the atomic Hardy spaces introduced in [50, p. 592]. In this monograph,
the notion of indices is going to play a fundamental role in the formulation and proofs of
many of our main results. For more information about this lower smoothness index, we
refer to [168, Section 4.7]; see also [7, Section 2.5].
Definition 1.1.3. Let (X, q) be a quasi-ultrametric space and ρ ∈q. The lower smoothness
index ind (X, q) [resp. ind (X, ρ)] of (X, q) [resp. (X, ρ)] is defined by setting
ind (X, q) := sup

The following example highlights the fact that the inequality in (1.1.10) can be strict.
The reader is referred to Comment 4.38 and Remark 4.49 in [168] for more details.
Example 1.1.9. Let a, b, c, and d be four real numbers with the property that a < b <
c < d. Then ind ([a, b] ∪[c, d], | · −· |) = 1 and indH([a, b] ∪[c, d], | · −· |) = ∞.
Recall that a topological space (X, τ) is said to be metrizable if there exists a met-
ric d on X satisfying that the topology induced by d on X coincides with τ.
There
are several metrization results in various branches of mathematics, such as the Mac´ıas–
Segovia metrization theorem (see Theorem 1.1.10 below) for quasi-metric spaces (har-
monic analysis), the Alexandroff–Urysohn metrization theorem (see, for instance, [168,
Theorem 1.1] and [170, Theorem 34.1]) for uniform spaces (topology), and the Aoki–
Rolewicz theorem (see, for instance, [14, Theorem 5] and [185]) for quasi-normed vector
spaces (functional analysis). Here, we focus on the contribution of Mac´ıas and Segovia
[160] which has been hailed as a cornerstone of the analysis on quasi-metric spaces. We
record it as follows; see [160, Theorem 2].
Theorem 1.1.10 (Mac´ıas–Segovia). Let (X, ρ) be a quasi-metric space; that is, X is a
nonempty set and ρ : X×X →R+ is a quasi-metric [a function satisfies Definition 1.1.1(i),
Definition 1.1.1(ii) with C0 = 1, and (1.1.3)]. Then there exists another quasi-metric ϱ on
X satisfying that
(i) ρ ∼ϱ;
(ii) ϱα is a metric on X that induces the same topology on X as ρ, where
α :=

In particular, this topology on X is metrizable;
(iii) ϱ satisfies the following H¨older-type regularity condition of order α; that is, for any
x, y, z ∈X,
|ϱ(x, z) −ϱ(y, z)| ≤1

Until now, Theorem 1.1.10 has still played a key role in analysis on spaces of ho-
mogeneous type because the natural setting for analysis in this context is the setting of
quasi-metric spaces. Via Theorem 1.1.10, a considerable portion of the well-established
results in the area of analysis on metric spaces may be quite successfully developed in the
more general setting of quasi-metric spaces. Indeed, quasi-metric spaces are metrizable,
but it is a rather subtle matter to associate metrics, inducing the same topology, in a way
that brings out the quantitative features of the quasi-metric space in question in an optimal
manner. The seminal work on this topic done by R. Mac´ıas and C. Segovia has been very
influential in the study of spaces of homogeneous type; see, for example, the monographs
by Christ [42], by Stein [197], by Triebel [206], by Heinonen [108], by Han and Sawyer
[100], by David and Semmes [60], and by Deng and Han [61].
Next, we recall a sharp metrization result of Mitrea et al. [168], which is a sharpened
version of Theorem 1.1.10. To this end, we begin with recalling some notions related
to the quasi-ultrametric ρ, which is useful to construct the metric we needed; see, for
instance, [7, pp. 36–37] and [168, Theorem 3.46].

In light of Lemma 1.1.14, we record the following definition for a regularized quasi-
ultrametric; see [168, Theorem 3.26] and [7, Theorem 2.1].
Definition 1.1.16. Let (X, q) be a quasi-ultrametric space, ρ ∈q, and αρ := (log2 Cρ)−1 ∈
(0, ∞], where Cρ is the same as in (1.1.1). The quasi-ultrametric ρ# := (ρsym)αρ is called the
regularized quasi-ultrametric (of ρ). More generally, ϱ ∈q is referred to as a regularized
quasi-ultrametric if ϱ is the regularized quasi-ultrametric of some ϱ′ ∈q.

Remark 1.1.17. Let the notation be as in Definition 1.1.16.
(i) If ρ is a metric on X and αρ = 1 (namely Cρ = 2), then ρ# := (ρsym)αρ = ρ. In
particular, ρ# = ρ if ρ is the standard Euclidean distance on Rn. Moreover, if ρ is a
regularized quasi-ultrametric then ρ# = ρ.
(ii) By Lemma 1.1.14(i), we find that the regularized quasi-ultrametric of any quasi-
ultrametric satisfying Definition 1.1.1(iii) with C1 := 1 is precisely its symmetriza-
tion. In particular, the regularized quasi-ultrametric of any ultrametric is exactly
itself.
(iii) From Remark 1.1.4(iii), (1.1.8), and Lemma 1.1.14, we infer that, for any 0 <
ε ⪯ind (X, q), there exists a regularized quasi-ultrametric ρ ∈q satisfying ε ≤
(log2 Cρ)−1.
(iv) The advantage of the regularized quasi-ultrametric is that it guarantees the mea-
surability of certain functions (which are defined via such a regularized quasi-
ultrametric), such as the Hardy–Littlewood maximal function (see Lemma 1.2.9),
the Lusin area function (see Lemma 3.1.3), and the non-tangential maximal func-
tion (see Proposition 5.1.7).
In order to extend certain classical results of function spaces over Euclidean spaces
to more general settings, we need to introduce some measure theoretic notions, begin-
ning with the definition of quasi-ultrametric spaces of homogeneous type in the sense of
Coifman and Weiss [49, 50].
Definition 1.1.18. A triplet (X, ρ, µ) is called a quasi-ultrametric space of homogeneous
type if (X, ρ) is a quasi-ultrametric space and µ is a nonnegative measure on X that satisfies
the following doubling condition (with respect to ρ): all ρ-balls are µ-measurable and there
exists a constant Cµ ∈[1, ∞) such that, for any x ∈X and r ∈(0, ∞),
0 < µ Bρ(x, 2r) ≤Cµµ Bρ(x, r) < ∞.
(1.1.18)
If ρ is a metric, then (X, ρ, µ) is called a doubling metric measure space . Finally, the
triplet (X, q, µ) is called a quasi-ultrametric space of homogeneous type if (X, ρ, µ) is a
quasi-ultrametric space of homogeneous type for some ρ ∈q.
Remark 1.1.19. Let (X, ρ, µ) be a quasi-ultrametric space of homogeneous type.
(i) It follows from the doubling condition (1.1.18) that, for any λ ∈[1, ∞), x ∈X, and
r ∈(0, ∞),
µ Bρ(x, λr) ≤Cµλnµ Bρ(x, r),

where n := log2 Cµ is called the upper dimension of X. Moreover, if the cardinality
of the set X is at least 2, then, by (1.1.18) again, we find that Cµ ∈(1, ∞) and hence
n ∈(0, ∞); see [7, p. 72].
(ii) It is known that, under the assumption that µ(Bρ(x, r)) ∈(0, ∞) for any x ∈X
and r ∈(0, ∞), diamρ(X) = ∞if and only if µ(X) = ∞(see, for instance, [179,
Lemma 5.1] and [218, p. 7]).

(iii) We refer to [50, pp. 588–590] for several examples of quasi-ultrametric spaces of
homogeneous type.
Remark 1.1.20. If (X, q, µ) is a quasi-ultrametric space of homogeneous type, then the
measure µ satisfies the doubling condition (1.1.18) with respect to any other ρ′ ∈q for
which all ρ′-balls are µ-measurable; see Proposition 1.1.32 below.
The following lemma is precisely [126, Lemma 2.1(i)]; see also [160, Theorem 1].
Lemma 1.1.21. Let (X, ρ, µ) be a quasi-ultrametric space of homogeneous type. Then,
for any x ∈X, µ({x}) ∈(0, ∞) if and only if there exists r ∈(0, ∞) such that {x} = Bρ(x, r).

The stage has now been set to introduce the notion of an ultra-RD-space in the follow-
ing definition.
Definition 1.1.24. A triplet (X, ρ, µ) is called a (κ, n)-ultra-RD-space (or simply ultra-RD-
space) if (X, ρ, µ) is a quasi-ultrametric space of homogeneous type with upper dimension
n ∈(0, ∞) in (1.1.19) and if the measure µ satisfies the following additional reverse
doubling condition (with respect to ρ): there exist four constants C1, c1 ∈(0, 1], c2 ∈
[1, ∞), and κ ∈(0, n] such that, for any x ∈X, r ∈(0, ∞) ∩[c1rρ(x), c2Rρ(x)], and

where rρ(x) and Rρ(x) are as, respectively, in (1.1.20) and (1.1.21). A triplet (X, q, µ) is
called a (κ, n)-ultra-RD-space if (X, ρ, µ) is a (κ, n)-ultra-RD-space for some ρ ∈q.
Remark 1.1.25.
(i) In 2004, Nagel and Stein [172] developed the product theory on
Carnot–Carath´eodory spaces with a quasi-ultrametric ρ and a measure µ satisfying
both the doubling and the reverse doubling conditions (1.1.18) and (1.1.22). This
reverse doubling property was further extended by Han et al. [98, 99] into metric
measure spaces. Such quasi-ultrametric spaces of homogeneous type having the
reverse-doubling property are called RD-spaces, which were originally introduced
in [99]. Recall that an RD-space in [99] is a doubling metric measure space (X, ρ, µ),
where the measure µ satisfies the following additional condition: there exist two
constants e

By Remark 1.1.23, we find that these RD-spaces are special cases of ultra-RD-
spaces in Definition 1.1.24 where ρ is a metric and rρ(x) = 0 for any x ∈X. For
further studies about RD-spaces, we refer to [98, 99, 230].
(ii) We point out that the condition that r ≥c1rρ(x) in Definition 1.1.24 is necessary if
X has the property that the measure of a singleton is strictly positive. However, as it
was shown in [160, p. 259, Theorem 1], there can only be at most countably many
such points.
(iii) There are many examples of ultra-RD-spaces, such as Euclidean spaces with A∞-
weights (Muckenhoupt’s class), Ahlfors-regular spaces, Lie groups of polynomial
growth, and Carnot–Carath´eodory spaces with doubling measure.

Remark 1.1.26. If (X.q, µ) is an ultra-RD-space, then the measure µ satisfies the reverse
doubling condition (1.1.22) with respect to any other quasi-ultrametric ρ′ ∈q for which
all ρ′-balls are µ-measurable; see Proposition 1.1.37 below.
Now, we recall the notion of Ahlfors-regular quasi-ultrametric spaces (see, for instance,

Definition 1.1.27. Let n ∈(0, ∞). A triplet (X, ρ, µ) is called an n-Ahlfors-regular quasi-
ultrametric space if (X, ρ) is a quasi-ultrametric space and µ is a nonnegative measure on X
with the property that all ρ-balls are µ-measurable and there exist c1 ∈(0, 1], c2 ∈[1, ∞),
and C1,C2 ∈(0, ∞) such that the following n-Ahlfors-regular condition holds: for any
x ∈X and r ∈(0, ∞) satisfying r ∈[c1rρ(x), c2Rρ(x)],
C1rn ≤µ Bρ(x, r) ≤C2rn,
(1.1.23)
where rρ(x) and Rρ(x) are the same as, respectively, in (1.1.20) and (1.1.21). A triplet
(X, q, µ) is called an n-Ahlfors-regular quasi-ultrametric space if (X, ρ, µ) is an n-Ahlfors-
regular quasi-ultrametric space for some ρ ∈q.
Remark 1.1.28.
(i) Recall that a triplet (X, ρ, µ) is called an n-Ahlfors-regular metric
space, with n ∈(0, ∞), if ρ is a metric and there exists a constant C ∈[1, ∞)
such that, for any x ∈X and r ∈(0, diamρ(X)), C−1rn ≤µ(Bρ(x, r)) ≤Crn. By
Remark 1.1.23, we find that n-Ahlfors-regular metric spaces are special cases of the
n-Ahlfors-regular quasi-ultrametric spaces in Definition 1.1.27 when ρ is a metric
and rρ(x) = 0 for any x ∈X.
(ii) It is clear from definitions that the n-Ahlfors-regularity condition (1.1.23) implies
the reverse doubling condition (1.1.22) with κ = n. Moreover, it can be shown that
(1.1.23) also implies that the doubling condition (1.1.19) holds; see, for instance,
[7, Proposition 2.12]. Therefore, an n-Ahlfors-regular quasi-ultrametric space is
an (n, n)-ultra-RD-space and hence a quasi-ultrametric space of homogeneous type
with upper dimension n.

Historically, assuming a certain degree of regularity for the underlying measure has
proven extremely useful in establishing a number of fundamental results used in the anal-
ysis on spaces of homogeneous type, such as the density of smooth functions in Lebesgue
spaces and the Lebesgue differentiation theorem. It turns out that the sharp regularity
condition on the underlying measure is the notion of Borel-semiregularity introduced in
[7, Definition 3.9]. We now take a moment to record this concept here as it will be of cen-
tral importance throughout this monograph. To this end, in what follows, for an arbitrary
set X equipped with a topology τ, we denote by Borelτ(X) the smallest σ-algebra on X
containing τ. We now recall several related notions of measure regularity in the following
definition (see, for instance, [7, Definitions 2.9 and 3.9] and [108, p. 3]).
Definition 1.1.30. Let X be a set equipped with a topology τ, and let M be a σ-algebra of
subsets of X. Assume that µ : M →[0, ∞] is a nonnegative measure.
(i) µ is called a Borel measure on (X, τ) if Borelτ(X) ⊂M.
(ii) µ is called a Borel-semiregular measure on (X, τ) if µ is a Borel measure on X and
satisfies that, for any E ∈M with µ(E) < ∞, there exists B ∈Borelτ(X) such that
µ(E∆B) = 0, where E∆B := (E \ B) ∪(B \ E).
(iii) µ is called a Borel-regular measure on (X, τ) if µ is a Borel measure on X and
satisfies that, for any E ∈M, there exists B ∈Borelτ(X) such that E ⊂B and
µ(E) = µ(B).
Remark 1.1.31. In regards to Definition 1.1.30, we have the following observations.
(i) Note that Zhou et al. [234] assumed that µ is a Borel regular measure and satisfies
that the measure of every singleton is zero. Moreover, by (ii) and (iii) of Defini-
tion 1.1.30, it is easy to see that, if µ is a Borel-regular measure on (X, τ), then µ is
a Borel-semiregular measure on (X, τ). Thus, all the results of this monograph are
done under some weaker assumptions of µ than those in [234].
(ii) By Whitney’s decomposition theorem for open sets (see [6, Theorem 6.2], [7, The-
orem 2.4], and Lemma 5.2.11 in this monograph; see also Remark 1.1.41(iii) in
this regard) every open subset of a quasi-ultrametric space of homogeneous type
(X, q, µ) can be decomposed into a countable union of ρ-balls, each of which is
µ-measurable, since ρ ∈q in Definition 1.1.18 is chosen so that all ρ-balls are
µ-measurable.
Consequently, every doubling measure is automatically a Borel
measure. In particular, if ϱ ∈q is any regularized quasi-ultrametric as in Def-
inition 1.1.16, then, all ϱ-balls are open, and thus we find that all ϱ-balls are µ-
measurable.
(iii) We stress here that, for µ : M →[0, ∞] to be a Borel measure, we only require that
Borelτ(X) is a subset of M and not necessarily that Borelτ(X) = M. In the latter
case, the measure µ would automatically be Borel-regular.

Corollary 1.1.33. Let (X, q, µ) be a quasi-ultrametric space of homogeneous type and fix
ρ ∈q. Let ρ# be the regularized quasi-ultrametric of ρ as in Definition 1.1.16 and ρsym
the symmetrization of ρ as in Definition 1.1.11(ii). Then (X, ρ#, µ) is a quasi-ultrametric
space of homogeneous type. Moreover, if all ρ-balls are µ-measurable, then (X, ρsym, µ) is
also a quasi-ultrametric space of homogeneous type.
Some basic estimates are stated in the following lemma, which is a generalization of
[98, Lemma 2.1] (see also [103, Lemma 2.4]) from doubling metric measure spaces to
quasi-ultrametric spaces of homogeneous type and whose proof remains true for quasi-
ultrametric spaces of homogeneous type. Indeed, recall that, in contrast to doubling metric
measure spaces, for the quasi-ultrametric space of homogeneous type in this monograph,
we have no restriction on µ({x}) for any x ∈X, which means that it could happen that
either µ({x}) = 0 or µ({x}) > 0. If, for some x ∈X, µ({x}) > 0, then, by [160, Theorem 1],

Corollary 1.1.38. Let (X, q, µ) be a (κ, n)-ultra-RD-space with 0 < κ ≤n < ∞and
fix ρ ∈q. Let ρ# ∈q be the regularized quasi-ultrametric of ρ as in Definition 1.1.16
and ρsym ∈q be the symmetrization of ρ as in Definition 1.1.11(ii). Then (X, ρ#, µ) is a

The following corollary on Ahlfors-regular quasi-ultrametric spaces is a direct conse-
quence of Propositions 1.1.32 and 1.1.37 (see also [7, Proposition 2.12]); we omit the
details.
Corollary 1.1.39. Let (X, q, µ) be an n-Ahlfors-regular quasi-ultrametric space with n ∈
(0, ∞) and fix ρ ∈q. Let ρ# ∈q be the regularized quasi-ultrametric of ρ as in Defini-
tion 1.1.16 and ρsym the symmetrization of ρ as in Definition 1.1.11(ii). Then (X, ρ#, µ) is
an n-Ahlfors-regular quasi-ultrametric space. Moreover, if all ρ-balls are µ-measurable,
then (X, ρsym, µ) is also an n-Ahlfors-regular quasi-ultrametric space. Furthermore, for
any given ρ′ ∈q, if all ρ′-balls are µ-measurable, then (X, ρ′, µ) is an n-Ahlfors-regular
quasi-ultrametric space.
Next, we recall the concepts of geometrically doubling quasi-ultrametric spaces and
systems of dyadic cubes on geometrically doubling quasi-ultrametric spaces.
Definition 1.1.40. A quasi-ultrametric space (X, ρ) is said to be geometrically doubling
(with respect to ρ) if there exists a constant N ∈N, called the geometric doubling constant
of (X, ρ), such that any ρ-ball of radius r ∈(0, ∞) in X can be covered by at most N ρ-
balls in X of radii r
2. Moreover, a quasi-ultrametric space (X, q) is called a geometrically
doubling quasi-ultrametric space if (X, ρ) is a geometrically doubling quasi-ultrametric
space for some ρ ∈q and, in this case, the constant N ∈N is called the geometric doubling
constant of (X, q) with respect to ρ.
Remark 1.1.41.
(i) A quasi-ultrametric space (X, q) is a geometrically doubling quasi-
ultrametric space if and only if, for any ρ ∈q and θ ∈(0, 1), there exists N(θ) ∈N,
depending on θ, such that any ρ-ball of radius r ∈(0, ∞) in X can be covered by at
most N(θ) ρ-balls in X of radii θr; see also [7, (2.34)]. This ensures that the definition
of (X, q) as a geometrically doubling quasi-ultrametric space in Definition 1.1.40 is
meaningful.
(ii) By [7, (2.35)], we find that, if (X, q) is a geometrically doubling quasi-ultrametric
space, then the topological space (X, τq) is separable, which is a consequence of the
geometrically doubling property.
(iii) Any quasi-ultrametric space of homogeneous type (X, q, µ) is a geometrically dou-
bling quasi-ultrametric space; see [7, Proposition 3.28] and [49].
We now recall the following construction given by Hyt¨onen and Kairema in [119, The-
orem 2.2], which provides an analogue of the grid of Euclidean dyadic cubes on geomet-
rically doubling quasi-ultrametric spaces; see also Christ [41, Theorem 11] and Hofmann
et al. [115, Proposition 2.11].

A Maximally Smooth Approximation of the Identity
In this section, let (X, q, µ) be a quasi-ultrametric space of homogeneous type. We
aim to build a maximally smooth approximation of the identity with bounded support on
(X, q, µ) and show some properties of this brand of approximation of the identity. As a

The choice to denote the weak Lebesgue space by Lp,∞(X) is due to the fact that the weak
Lebesgue space may be viewed as a particular example of a more general scale of function
spaces, namely Lorentz spaces Lp,q(X), which we will introduce in Section 5.1. In that
setting, the identity Lp,∞(X) = weak-Lp(X) will become clear. For now, we simply adopt
this notation with the understanding that weak Lebesgue spaces are a distinct object in
their own right, independent of the Lorentz spaces to be discussed later. For p ∈(0, ∞),
the symbol Lp
loc(X) denotes the space of all p-locally integrable functions on X. That
is, Lp
loc(X) is the set of all µ-measurable functions f : X →C such that, for any point
x ∈X, there exists a neighborhood of x where f is p-integrable; see, for instance, [108,
p. 5]. In what follows, 1-locally integrable functions will simply be referred to as locally
integrable.

Combining this and Lemma 1.1.14(ii), we deduce that, if {St}t∈(0,∞) is an approxi-
mation of the identity of order ε ∈(0, ∞), then its integral kernels still satisfy (i)–(v)
of Definition 1.2.1 with ρ replaced by any ρ′ ∈q having the property that all ρ′-balls
are µ-measurable. In particular, the kernels of {St}t∈(0,∞) still satisfy (i)–(v) of Defi-
nition 1.2.1 with ρ replaced by its regularized quasi-ultrametric ρ#. As such, without
loss of generality, we can always assume that ρ is a regularized quasi-ultrametric as
in Definition 1.2.1 in the remainder of this monograph.
(iv) By possibly increasing the constants C,C′ ∈(0, ∞) in Definition 1.2.1, we may
assume that C,C′ ∈[1, ∞).
(v) In order to defray our notation and simplify our presentation, we always assume
that θ = 1 in Definition 1.2.1 throughout this monograph.
Now, we present the main result of this section in the following theorem, which shows
the existence of the above approximation of the identity with its order in a maximal range.
To prove it, we borrow some ideas from the proof of [7, Theorem 3.22]; see also the proof
of [168, Theorem 4.93]. The reader is reminded of the significance of the symbol ⪯in
Remark 1.1.4(iii).
Theorem 1.2.3. Let (X, q, µ) be a quasi-ultrametric space of homogeneous type. Then,
for any ε0 ∈(0, ∞) satisfying 0 < ε0 ⪯ind (X, q) with ind (X, q) as in (1.1.8), there exists
an approximation of the identity {St}t∈(0,∞) of any order ε ∈(0, ε0].

{St}t∈(0,∞) (with θ = 1 in Definition 1.2.1) of any order ε ∈(0, ε0] in the case both
ε0 ∈(0, 1) and ε0 ⪯ind (X, q). To this end, we follow Coifman’s idea (see the dis-
cussion on [59, pp. 16–17 and p. 40]) with the goal of monitoring the maximal amount
of H¨older regularity for the integral kernels on quasi-ultrametric spaces of homogeneous
type; see also [162, Theorem (1.13)]. By (1.1.8), we find that there exists ρ ∈q satisfy-
ing that ε0 ∈(0, (log2 Cρ)−1]. Let ρ# ∈q be the regularized quasi-ultrametric of ρ as in

Remark 1.2.4.
(i) From the proof of (1.2.7) and Definition 1.2.1, it follows that, for
any {St}t∈(0,∞) as in Definition 1.2.1, t ∈(0, ∞), and x, y, x′ ∈X,
|S t(x, y) −S t(x′, y)| ≲
"ρ(x, x′)
t

1
(Vρ)t(y).
(ii) The proof of Theorem 1.2.3 relies heavily on the properties of the quasi-ultrametric
as defined in Definition 1.1.1 and on the quasi-ultrametric space structure q. More
precisely, the reason why the order ε of the approximation of the identity can be
extended to such a wide range is because of Lemma 1.1.14(iii) and Lemma 1.1.5.
On the one hand, Lemma 1.1.14(iii) makes it possible that ε can be bigger than
1 and even belong to (0, ∞) if we choose some special q. On the other hand,
Lemma 1.1.5 helps us to extend the range of ε ∈(0, 1] to 0 < ε ⪯ind (X, q) when-
ever ind (X, q) ∈(1, ∞]. However, unlike the constant Cρ in (1.1.1), the constant Aρ
in the quasi-triangle inequality as in Remark 1.1.2(iv) does not have the property
that (Aρ)α = Aρα for any α ∈(0, ∞) and hence a conclusion similar to Lemma 1.1.5
for quasi-metrics does not hold. From this perspective, it is crucial for us to work
with the quasi-ultrametric inequality instead of the quasi-metric inequality in order
to achieve the full strength of our results.
(iii) Recall that, if (X, ρ, µ) is a doubling metric measure space, then there always ex-
ists an approximation of the identity with any order ε ∈(0, 1] (see, for instance,
[99, Theorem 2.6]). By Theorem 1.2.3, we find that, if q contains a metric, then
ind (X, q) ∈[1, ∞] and hence, for any given 0 < ε0 ⪯ind (X, q), there always exists
an approximation of the identity of any order ε ∈(0, ε0] and that, if q contains an
ultrametric, which is a special case of metrics, then ind (X, q) = ∞and hence, for
any given ε0 ∈(0, ∞), there always exists an approximation of the identity of any
order ε ∈(0, ε0]. Thus, Theorem 1.2.3 is sharper than [99, Theorem 2.6].
(iv) By the proof of Theorem 1.2.3, we find that Theorem 1.2.3 holds in a more gen-
eral measure theoretic setting in which the measure of a singleton is allowed to be
strictly positive.
(v) We point out that [7, Definition 3.21 and Theorem 3.22] are, respectively, special
cases of Definition 1.2.1 and Theorem 1.2.3 when (X, q, µ) is an Ahlfors-regular
quasi-ultrametric space.

Next, we show that St f is a µ-measurable function. If λ ∈R is such that
{x ∈X : St f(x) > λ} = X or {x ∈X : St f(x) > λ} = ∅,
then there is nothing to prove. Now, assume that λ ∈R is such that {x ∈X : St f(x) > λ} is
a proper and nonempty subset of X. Moreover, by the above proof, we find that {x ∈X :
St f(x) > λ} is open. Since (X, q, µ) is a quasi-ultrametric space of homogeneous type, it
follows from [7, Proposition 3.28] [see also Remark 1.1.41(iii) below] that (X, q) is also
a geometrically doubling quasi-ultrametric space as in Definition 1.1.40. By this and the
Whitney decomposition theorem for open sets [7, Theorem 2.4] [see also Lemma 5.2.11(i)
below], we find that the open set {x ∈X : St f(x) > λ} can be decomposed into a countable
union of ρ-balls, which are obviously µ-measurable because the choice of ρ ∈q is such
that all ρ-balls are µ-measurable. This finishes the proof of Proposition 1.2.5.
□
In the next main result of this section (Theorem 1.2.7), we collect some important prop-
erties that are satisfied by any approximation of the identity defined in Definition 1.2.1.

this monograph, given a function f : X →C, the support of f is defined by setting
supp f := {x ∈X : f(x) , 0} .

For the approximation of the identity defined in Definition 1.2.1, we have the following
conclusions.
Theorem 1.2.7. Let (X, q, µ) be a quasi-ultrametric space of homogeneous type. Assume
that {St}t∈(0,∞) is an approximation of the identity of order ε ∈(0, ∞). Then the following
statements hold:
(i) For any p ∈[1, ∞], supt∈(0,∞) ∥St∥Lp(X)→Lp(X) < ∞.
(ii) supt∈(0,∞) ∥St∥˙Cε(X)→˙Cε(X) < ∞.
(iii) For any g ∈˙Cε(X) and α ∈(0, ε), limt→0+ ∥Stg −g∥˙Cα(X) = 0.
(iv) supt∈(0,∞) ∥St∥Cε(X)→Cε(X) < ∞.
(v) For any g ∈Cε(X) and α ∈(0, ε), limt→0+ ∥Stg −g∥Cα(X) = 0.
(vi) There exists a constant C ∈(0, ∞) such that, for any t ∈(0, ∞) and f ∈L∞(X),
∥St f∥˙Cε(X) ≤Ct−ε ∥f∥L∞(X) .
(vii) If diam (X) = ∞, then, for any given p ∈(1, ∞) and for any f ∈Lp(X),
lim
t→∞∥St f∥Lp(X) = 0.
(viii) Let ρ0 ∈q, x0 ∈X, and r0 ∈(0, ∞). If f ∈L1
loc(X) is such that supp f ⊂Bρ0(x0, r0),
then there exists a positive constant C, independent of f, x0, and r0, such that, for
any t ∈(0, ∞), supp St f ⊂Bρ0(x0,C(r0 + t)).

Remark 1.2.8.
(i) In the proof of Theorem 1.2.7(i), we find that the supremum in
Theorem 1.2.7(i) is dominated by a constant independent of p ∈[1, ∞].
(ii) The Borel-semiregular assumption on µ in Theorem 1.2.7(ix) is weaker than that
of [99, Proposition 2.7] in which µ is assumed to be Borel-regular. Indeed, Theo-
rem 1.2.7(ix) is sharp due to Lemma 1.2.10.
(iii) If µ is a Borel measure on X, then Theorem 1.2.7 holds with the same proof except
(1.2.20). In this case, (1.2.20) holds only for any p ∈[1, ∞] and f belonging to the
closure of ˙Cε(X) ∩Lp(X) in Lp(X).

any f ∈Lp(X). Let f ∈Lp(X). In light of Lemma 1.2.10, the goal is to use (1.2.20)
to approximate f arbitrarily well in Lp(X) with functions from Cb(X). To this end, since
simple functions are dense in Lp(X), without loss of generality, we may assume that f is
a characteristic function, 1E, for some µ-measurable set E ⊂X with µ(E) < ∞. We can
also assume that E is actually bounded. Indeed, if we take any point x0 ∈X, then, for
any j ∈N, E j := E ∩Bρ(x0, j) is bounded and µ-measurable and satisfies that 1E j →1E
in Lp(X) as j →∞. Since E is bounded, it follows that 1E has bounded support. By this
and (viii), we find that, for any t ∈(0, ∞), St1E also has bounded support. From this, (vi),
and the fact that 1E ∈L∞(X), we infer that, for any t ∈(0, ∞), St1E ∈Cε
b(X) ⊂Cb(X).
By (1.2.20), we conclude that St1E →1E in Lp(X) as t →0+ and, consequently, 1E
is approximated arbitrarily well in Lp(X) by functions from Cb(X), and we ultimately
deduce that Cb(X) is dense in Lp(X). From this and Lemma 1.2.10, we further infer that
µ is Borel-semiregular, which completes the proof of the necessity and hence (ix). This
finishes the proof of Theorem 1.2.7.

## Page 56


Chapter 2
Sharp Calder´on Reproducing Formulae
on Ultra-RD-Spaces
The main goal of this chapter is to use the approximation of the identity from Def-
inition 1.2.1 to construct sharp homogeneous continuous/discrete Calder´on reproducing
formulae (as well as their inhomogeneous counterparts) on the ultra-RD-space (X, q, µ),
where µ is assumed to be Borel-semiregular on X. Indeed, this is an improvement over
works in [99] and [103] in the sense that these Calder´on reproducing formulae obtained
in this section are sharp with respect to the regularity exponent in the setting of ultra-
RD-spaces. To this end, in Section 2.1, we first recall the definitions of spaces of test
functions and distributions on (X, q, µ) and present some basic estimates. In Section 2.2,
we first recall the definitions of Calder´on–Zygmund kernels and Calder´on–Zygmund op-
erators, and then establish the boundedness of Calder´on–Zygmund operators on spaces
of test functions with (or without) cancellation. Finally, we establish the sharp homo-
geneous continuous Calder´on reproducing formula in Section 2.3, the sharp inhomoge-
neous continuous Calder´on reproducing formula in Section 2.4, the sharp homogeneous
discrete Calder´on reproducing formula in Section 2.5, and the sharp inhomogeneous dis-
crete Calder´on reproducing formula in Section 2.6, all of which hold, respectively, in
spaces of test functions, spaces of distributions, and Lp(X) with p ∈(1, ∞).
2.1
Test Function and Distribution Spaces
In this section, we first recall the notions of test functions and distributions on quasi-
ultrametric spaces of homogeneous type, and then state some related estimates that will
be frequently used in the sequel. To this end, we first recall the definition of spaces of
test functions, which was originally introduced by Han et al. [99, Definition 2.8]; see also
[98, Definition 2.2].
Definition 2.1.1. Let (X, q, µ) be a quasi-ultrametric space of homogeneous type, where
µ satisfies the doubling condition (1.1.18) with respect to ρ ∈q. Fix x1 ∈X, r ∈(0, ∞),
β ∈(0, ∞), and γ ∈(0, ∞). A µ-measurable function f : X →C is called a test function
of type (x1, r, β, γ), denoted by f ∈G(x1, r, β, γ), if there exists a positive constant C such
that

Remark 2.1.2. Let the notation be as in Definition 2.1.1.
(i) Fix x1 ∈X. It can be seen that, for any x ∈X and r ∈(0, ∞),
G(x, r, β, γ) = G(x1, 1, β, γ)
with equivalent norms, but the positive equivalence constants depend on both x and
r. In what follows, we simply write
G(β, γ) := G(x1, 1, β, γ) and ˚G(β, γ) := ˚G(x1, 1, β, γ).
(ii) If ρ′ ∈q is any other quasi-ultrametric with the property that all ρ′-balls are µ-
measurable, then the volume functions defined by ρ′ and ρ are equivalent and hence
the spaces G(β, γ), constructed with respect to ρ or ρ′, are the same (with equivalent
norms). In this sense, G(β, γ) is independent of the choice of ρ ∈q. Throughout
this monograph, we always assume that G(β, γ) is constructed by using a regularized
quasi-ultrametric belonging to q.
(iii) G(β, γ) is a Banach space with respect to the norm in G(β, γ); see, for instance, [99,
p. 19].
(iv) The spaces G(β, γ) are nested with respect to β and γ in the sense that, for any
0 < β1 ≤β2 < ∞and 0 < γ1 ≤γ2 < ∞,
G(β2, γ2) ⊂G(β1, γ1)
with appropriate control of the norms.

In particular, if indH(X, q) < β < ∞, where indH(X, q) denotes the H¨older index of

(vi) As pointed out in [99, p. 18], Definition 2.1.1 gives a quantified meaning to the
notion of a sufficiently “smooth” function on quasi-ultrametric spaces of homoge-
neous type. To be more precise, Definition 2.1.1(i) means that such test functions
are essentially supported in Bρ(x1, r) in the sense that it decays of sufficiently high
order (measured by γ) at infinity and Definition 2.1.1(ii) means that such test func-
tions are H¨older continuous of order β (at the right scale r).

vening in the definition of ˚G(x1, r, β, γ) is well defined.
The following lemma shows that the space of test functions G(β, γ) is non-trivial when-
ever 0 < β ⪯ind (X, q) and γ ∈(0, ∞).

where X = Rn and ρ is the standard Euclidean distance on Rn, G(β2, γ) is not dense in

overcome this defect, we now recall the following definition of spaces of test functions;

The following result is a quantitative version of the classical Urysohn’s lemma, which
was originally proved in [168, Theorem 4.12] and subsequently generalized in [6, Theo-
rem 4.1].
Lemma 2.1.6. Let (X, ρ) be a quasi-ultrametric space. Assume that ε ∈(0, (log2 Cρ)−1] ∩
(0, ∞) with Cρ ∈[1, ∞) as in (1.1.1). Suppose that F0, F1 ⊂X are two nonempty sets with
the property that distρ(F0, F1) > 0. Then there exist a constant θρ ∈(0, ∞) and a function
φ ∈Cε(X) such that
(i) For any x ∈X, φ(x) ∈[0, 1].
(ii) For any x ∈F0, φ(x) = 0.
(iii) For any x ∈F1, φ(x) = 1.
(iv) ∥φ∥˙Cε(X) ≤θρ[distρ(F0, F1)]−ε.
The following conclusion is a direct corollary of Lemma 2.1.6.
Corollary 2.1.7. Let (X, ρ) be a quasi-ultrametric space and ε ∈(0, (log2 Cρ)−1] ∩(0, ∞).
Let x ∈X and r ∈(0, ∞). Then, for any A ∈[Cρ, ∞), there exists a function f ∈Cε
b(X)
such that
(i) For any y ∈X,
1Bρ(x,r)(y) ≤f(y) ≤1Bρ(x,Ar)(y).
(ii) ∥f∥˙Cε(X) ≤Cr−ε, where C ∈(0, ∞) is independent of both x and r.
Remark 2.1.8. If ρ is a metric, then (log2 Cρ)−1 ∈[1, ∞] and Corollary 2.1.7 holds for
any β ∈(0, (log2 Cρ)−1]. If ρ is an ultrametric, then (log2 Cρ)−1 = ∞and Corollary 2.1.7
holds for any β ∈(0, ∞). However, He et al. [103, Lemma 3.3] only showed the existence
of such smooth cut-off functions as in Lemma 2.1.7 for any ε ∈(0, 1). In this sense,
Corollary 2.1.7 is sharper than [103, Lemma 3.3].

Finally, we recall the concept of spaces of distributions on quasi-ultrametric spaces of
homogeneous type; see [99, p. 19]. To this end, we introduce some notation: Given a
vector space Y (over C or similarly over R) and a quasi-norm ∥· ∥Y defined on Y, the
topological dual Y′ of Y is defined by setting
Y′ :=  f : (Y, τ∥·∥Y) →C is continuous and linear	 ,
where τ∥·∥Y is the topology induced by the quasi-norm ∥· ∥Y on Y. We can now state the
following definition.
Definition 2.1.12. Let (X, q, µ) be a quasi-ultrametric space of homogeneous type, where
µ satisfies the doubling condition (1.1.18) with respect to ρ ∈q. Assume that 0 < β, γ ≤
ε ⪯ind (X, q) with ind (X, q) as in (1.1.8). The dual space (Gε

It is well understood in the classical Euclidean setting that function f ∈Lr(Rn) with
r ∈[1, ∞] induces a distribution on D(Rn) := C∞
c (Rn) (denoted by Λ f) via integrating f
against any function from D(Rn) over the entire Rn. Indeed, this association is injective
and we may unambiguously identify such a distribution Λf with the function f itself.
As the following proposition asserts, this continues to remain valid in quasi-ultrametric
spaces of homogeneous type.

2.2
Boundedness of Calder´on–Zygmund Operators
on Spaces of Test Functions
In this section, we aim to establish the boundedness of Calder´on–Zygmund operators
on spaces of test functions. To this end, we first recall the definition of the Calder´on–
Zygmund kernel as follows.
Definition 2.2.1. Let (X, q, µ) be a quasi-ultrametric space of homogeneous type. Assume
that ρ ∈q and 0 < ε ⪯ind (X, q) with ind (X, q) as in (1.1.8). A function
K : (X × X) \ {(x, x) : x ∈X} →C
is called an ε-Calder´on–Zygmund kernel (with respect to ρ) if all ρ-balls are µ-measurable
and there exist two constants C∗∈(0, 1) and CT ∈(0, ∞) such that
(i) For any x, y ∈X with x , y,

Remark 2.2.2.
(i) Let 0 < ε ⪯ind (X, q) and K be an ε-Calder´on–Zygmund kernel
with respect to ρ as in Definition 2.2.1. Then K is also an ε-Calder´on–Zygmund
kernel with respect to any other quasi-ultrametric ρ′ ∈q for which all ρ′-balls are
µ-measurable. In particular, for the remainder of this monograph, without loss of
generality, we may always assume that ρ in Definition 2.2.1 is a regularized quasi-
ultrametric as in Definition 1.1.16.
(ii) In order to simplify the presentation of the calculations in this chapter, we assume
that C∗= (2Cρ)−1 in Definition 2.2.1, where Cρ is the same positive constant as in
(1.1.1). All of the proofs that use this assumption can be suitably modified for a
general constant C∗∈(0, 1).

Remark 2.2.7.
(i) We stress here that the cancellation condition of f is only used in

enough to establish Calder´on reproducing formulae.

Remark 2.2.9. The proof of Theorem 2.2.8 strongly relies on the existence of smooth cut-
off functions as in Corollary 2.1.7. Let T be an ε-Calder´on–Zygmund operator defined
as in Theorem 2.2.8. If ρ ∈q is a metric, then ind (X, q) ∈[1, ∞] and, for any β, γ ∈
(0, ind (X, q)), T is bounded on G(x1, r, β, γ), and, if ρ ∈q is an ultrametric, then, for
any β, γ ∈(0, ∞), T is bounded on G(x1, r, β, γ). However, in [103, Theorem 3.5], He
et al. only showed that T is bounded on G(x1, r, β, γ) with 0 < β, γ < 1. In this sense,
Theorem 2.2.8 is sharper than [103, Theorem 3.5].
2.3
Homogeneous Continuous Calder´on Reproducing
Formulae
The main goal of this section is to prove sharp homogeneous continuous Calder´on
reproducing formulae on ultra-RD-spaces (X, q, µ), where µ is assumed to be a Borel-
semiregular measure on X, which we present in Theorem 2.3.13. For any ρ ∈q, we
always assume that diamρ(X) = ∞. From this and Remark 1.1.23, it is easy to infer that
µ(X) = ∞and, for any ρ ∈q and x ∈X, Rρ(x) = ∞.
We begin with first introducing the following notion of homogeneous approximations
of the identity on quasi-ultrametric spaces of homogeneous type. The reader is reminded
that the symbol “⪯” was introduced in Remark 1.1.4(iii).
Definition 2.3.1. Let (X, q, µ) be a quasi-ultrametric space of homogeneous type and 0 <
ε ⪯ind (X, q) with ind (X, q) as in (1.1.8). A sequence {S2−k}k∈Z of linear operators is
called a homogeneous approximation of the identity of order ε if {S2−k}k∈Z satisfies all the
conditions of Definition 1.2.1 with t replaced by 2−k.
By Theorem 1.2.7, we have the following properties for any homogeneous approxima-
tion of the identity.
Proposition 2.3.2. Let (X, q, µ) be a quasi-ultrametric space of homogeneous type, where
µ is assumed to be a Borel-semiregular measure on X. Let 0 < ε ⪯ind (X, q) with
ind (X, q) as in (1.1.8) and {S2−k}k∈Z be a homogeneous approximation of the identity of
order ε as in Definition 2.3.1. Then the following assertions hold:

(i) For any p ∈(1, ∞) and f ∈Lp(X), ∥S2−k f∥Lp(X) →0 as k →−∞.
(ii) For any k ∈Z, let Dk := S2−k −S2−k+1. Then, for any p ∈(1, ∞),
∞

in Lp(X), where I is the identity operator on Lp(X).
Proof. Since diamρ(X) = ∞, (i) follows immediately from Theorem 1.2.7(vii), whereas
(ii) is a simple corollary of (i) and Theorem 1.2.7(ix). This finishes the proof of Proposi-
tion 2.3.2.
□
To establish the sharp homogeneous continuous Calder´on reproducing formulae, we
need several technical lemmas. The following conclusion follows directly from Defini-
tion 1.2.1 (see also Remark 1.2.2 and Lemma 2.1.3); we omit the details.

We are ready to establish the following sharp homogeneous continuous Calder´on re-
producing formula.
Theorem 2.3.13. Let (X, q, µ) be an ultra-RD-space, where µ is a Borel-semiregular mea-
sure on X. Assume that 0 < ε ⪯ind (X, q) with ind (X, q) as in (1.1.8) and {S2−k}k∈Z (with
kernels {S 2−k}k∈Z) is a homogeneous approximation of the identity of order ε in Defini-
tion 2.3.1. For any k ∈Z, let Dk := S2−k −S2−k+1. Then there exist two families {eDk}k∈Z
and {Dk}k∈Z of bounded linear operators on L2(X), with their kernels denoted, respec-
tively, by {eDk}k∈Z and {Dk}k∈Z, such that, for any f ∈˚Gε

Remark 2.3.14.
(i) In Theorem 2.3.13, for any k ∈Z, all the estimates satisfied by eDk
and Dk are independent of both β and γ with β, γ ∈(0, ε), but may depend on ε.
(ii) If ρ ∈q is a metric, then ind (X, q) ∈[1, ∞] and, for any 0 < β, γ < ε ⪯ind (X, q),
(2.3.58) holds in ˚Gε
0(β, γ); if ρ ∈q is an ultrametric, then, for any 0 < β, γ < ε < ∞,
(2.3.58) holds in ˚Gε
0(β, γ). However, Han et al. [99, Theorem 3.10] and He et al.
[103, Theorem 4.15] only showed homogeneous continuous Calder´on reproducing
formulae hold for any 0 < β, γ < ε < 1. In this sense, Theorem 2.3.13 is sharper
than [99, Theorem 3.10] and [103, Theorem 4.15] because of the sharpness of both
Theorems 1.2.3 and 2.2.5.

2.4
Inhomogeneous Continuous Calder´on Reproducing
Formulae
The main result of this section is Theorem 2.4.7, where we establish inhomogeneous
continuous Calder´on reproducing formulae on the ultra-RD-space (X, q, µ), where µ is
assumed to be a Borel-semiregular measure on X. We point out that, in contrast to the
homogeneous case, we have no restriction on diam (X), which means diam (X) < ∞or
diam (X) = ∞. As pointed out in [99, Remark 1.7], an essential difference in the theory
occurs when µ(X) < ∞[equivalently diam (X) < ∞] versus the case when µ(X) = ∞
[equivalently diam (X) = ∞].
This is because, when µ(X) < ∞, it is not true that
∥S2−k f∥Lp(X) →0 as k →−∞for any f ∈Lp(X) and for any given p ∈(1, ∞); see
the proof of Theorem 1.2.7(vii). Thus, when µ(X) < ∞, in the Calder´on reproducing for-
mula (2.3.58), we should replace D0 by S0 and Dk for any k ∈{−1, −2, . . .} by 0. Then,
with this modification, we always have an inhomogeneous term related to S0, which needs
some special care.
We first introduce the following definition of the inhomogeneous approximation of
the identity on X. The reader is reminded that the symbol “⪯” was introduced in Re-
mark 1.1.4(iii) and that Z+ := {0, 1, 2, . . . }.
Definition 2.4.1. Let (X, q, µ) be a quasi-ultrametric space of homogeneous type. Assume
that 0 < ε ⪯ind (X, q) with ind (X, q) as in (1.1.8). A sequence {S2−k}k∈Z+ of linear
operators is called an inhomogeneous approximation of the identity of order ε if {S2−k}k∈Z+
satisfies all the conditions of Definition 1.2.1 with t replaced by 2−k.
The following proposition for the inhomogeneous approximation of the identity is a
simple corollary of Theorem 1.2.7(ix); we omit the details of its proof.
Proposition 2.4.2. Let (X, q, µ) be a quasi-ultrametric space of homogeneous type, where
µ is assumed to be a Borel-semiregular measure on X. Assume that 0 < ε ⪯ind (X, q) with
ind (X, q) as in (1.1.8) and {S2−k}k∈Z+ is an inhomogeneous approximation of the identity
of order ε as in Definition 2.4.1. Let
D0 := S1 and Dk := S2−k −S2−k+1
for any k ∈N. Then, for any p ∈[1, ∞),
I =
∞
X
k=0
Dk
in Lp(X), where I is the identity operator on Lp(X).

To establish sharp inhomogeneous continuous Calder´on reproducing formulae, we
need a technical lemma which is a variant of Lemma 2.3.4.
Lemma 2.4.3. Let (X, q, µ) be a quasi-ultrametric space of homogeneous type, where µ
is assumed to be a Borel-semiregular measure on X. Assume that 0 < ε ⪯ind (X, q)
with ind (X, q) as in (1.1.8) and both {S2−k}k∈Z+ and {E2−k}k∈Z+ are two inhomogeneous
approximations of the identity of order ε defined as in Definition 2.4.1. For any k ∈N,
let Pk := S2−k −S2−k+1, Qk := E2−k −E2−k+1, P0 := S1, and Q0 := E1. Then, for any given
ε′ ∈(0, ε), δ ∈(0, ε−ε′], and ρ ∈q having the property that all ρ-balls are µ-measurable,
there exist two constants C,C′ ∈(0, ∞), depending on ε′, ε, and δ, such that, for any
k, l ∈Z+, (2.3.1), (2.3.2), (2.3.3), and (2.3.4) still hold.
Proof. We denote, respectively, by {S 2−k}k∈Z+ {E2−k}k∈Z+, {P2−k}k∈Z+, and {Q2−k}k∈Z+ the ker-
nels of {S2−k}k∈Z+, {E2−k}k∈Z+, {P2−k}k∈Z+, and {Q2−k}k∈Z+. Without loss of generality, we may
assume that ρ is a regularized quasi-ultrametric as in Definition 1.1.16. The proof of the
present lemma is essentially the same as that of Lemma 2.3.4. The only situations that are
different are the cases where l = 0 or k = 0. By symmetry, we only show (2.3.1), (2.3.2),
(2.3.3), and (2.3.4) in the case both k ∈Z+ and l = 0. For the case both k ∈N and l = 0,
the proofs of (2.3.1), (2.3.2), (2.3.3), and (2.3.4) are the same as that of Lemma 2.3.4
because we only need the vanish condition of Pk or Qk. Thus, to complete the proof of
the present lemma, it remains to prove (2.3.1), (2.3.2), (2.3.3), and (2.3.4) in the case
k = l = 0.
We first show (2.3.1). On the one hand, by the support conditions of both P0 = S 1 and
Q0 = E1 [see Definition 1.2.1(i)], we find that there exists a constant C ∈(1, ∞) such that,
for any x, y, z ∈X satisfying ρ(x, z) > C and ρ(y, z) > C,
P0(x, z) = 0 and Q0(z, y) = 0.
This, combined with Definition 1.1.1(iii), further implies that, for any x, y ∈X satisfying

together with the well-known Calder´on–Zygmund theory on spaces of homogeneous type
developed in [49, pp. 74–75, Theorem 2.4], further implies that eE(L) is bounded on Lp(X)
with its operator norm bounded by a positive constant independent of L. Applying this,
Lemma 2.1.10(ii), a standard density argument, and an argument similar to that used in
the proof of Theorem 2.3.13, we obtain the convergence of first series in (2.5.19) in Lp(X).
This finishes the proof of Step 3 and hence Theorem 2.5.7.

2.6
Inhomogeneous Discrete Calder´on Reproducing For-
mulae
The target of this section is to establish sharp inhomogeneous discrete Calder´on re-
producing formulae on the ultra-RD-space (X, q, µ), where µ is assumed to be a Borel-
semiregular measure on X. In particular, given ρ ∈q, we have no restriction on diamρ(X)
and hence we can have diamρ(X) < ∞or diamρ(X) = ∞. The proofs of the main results
of this section are similar to those presented in Sections 2.4 and 2.5. As such, we provide
a detailed account of their assertions and leave the details of the proofs to the reader.
Theorem 2.6.1. Let (X, q, µ) be an ultra-RD-space, where µ is assumed to be a Borel-
semiregular measure on X. Assume that 0 < ε ⪯ind (X, q) with ind (X, q) as in (1.1.8).
Let {S2−k}k∈Z+ be an inhomogeneous approximation of the identity of order ε as in Defini-
tion 2.4.1 (with kernels {S 2−k}k∈Z+). For any k ∈N, let
Dk := S2−k −S2−k+1 and D0 := S1.

By Theorem 2.6.1 and a standard duality argument, we obtain the following sharp
inhomogeneous discrete Calder´on reproducing formula on spaces of distributions; we

Chapter 3
Littlewood–Paley Function
Characterizations of Hardy Spaces on
Ultra-RD-Spaces
In [7], Alvarado and Mitrea introduced the Hardy spaces Hp
max(X) defined via the grand
maximal function on n-Ahlfors-regular quasi-ultrametric spaces (X, q, µ), with n ∈(0, ∞),
and proved, among other things, that this brand of Hardy spaces has atomic and molecular
characterizations for p ∈(
n
n+ind (X,q), 1] by showing that Hp
max(X) can be naturally identified
with the atomic Hardy space in this setting (which is known to coincide with the Coifman–
Weiss atomic Hardy space from [50]).
In this chapter, we continue the work initiated in [7] by establishing the Littlewood–
Paley characterizations of the Hardy spaces introduced in [7] in n-Ahlfors-regular quasi-
ultrametric spaces (see Remark 3.2.18). Indeed, we establish the Littlewood–Paley char-
acterizations of the Hardy spaces in a more general setting, namely (κ, n)-ultra-RD-spaces
(X, q, µ) with 0 < κ ≤n < ∞. To this end, in Section 3.1, we first recall the definitions
of both several kinds of Littlewood–Paley functions, based on the approximation of the
identity in Section 1.2, and the Hardy space Hp(X) defined via the Lusin area function for
the optimal range of p ∈(
n
n+ind (X,q), ∞). Then, using the homogeneous discrete Calder´on
reproducing formulae established in Section 2.5, we prove that, for any p ∈(
n
n+ind (X,q), ∞),
Hp(X) is independent of the choice of homogeneous approximations of the identity. In
Section 3.2, we recall the definition of the atomic Hardy space Hp
at(X) and show that Hp(X)
has an atomic characterization for p ∈(
n
n+ind (X,q), 1] via the identification Hp(X) = Hp
at(X).
We conclude this chapter with Section 3.3 wherein we also establish Littlewood–Paley g-
function and g∗
λ-function characterizations of Hp(X) with p ∈(
n
n+ind (X,q), 1]. In this chapter,
unless otherwise specified, we always assume that µ is a Borel-semiregular measure on X
and diamρ(X) = ∞for some ρ ∈q and hence diamρ(X) = ∞for all ρ ∈q.

lently, when X is bounded or unbounded.

The other main theorem of this section reads as follows, in which we consider the case
p ∈(1, ∞). The reader is reminded of Proposition 2.1.13.
Theorem 3.1.12. Let (X, q, µ) be an ultra-RD-space. Let p ∈(1, ∞) and β, γ, ε ∈(0, ∞)
satisfy (3.1.5). Then Hp(X) = Lp(X) in the following sense: the linear map Φ : Lp(X) →
Hp(X) that is defined by setting, for any f ∈Lp(X), Φ(f) := Λ f, where Λf ∈(Gε
0(β, γ))′
is the distribution defined in Proposition 2.1.13, is well defined, linear, bijective, and has
the property that, for any f ∈Lp(X),
∥f∥Lp(X) ∼
Λf
Hp(X) ,
where the positive equivalence constants are independent of f.
Remark 3.1.13. Similarly to that in Proposition 2.1.13, in the context of Theorem 3.1.12
since the association of f ∈Lp(X) to the distribution Λf ∈(Gε
0(β, γ))′ is injective whenever
µ is Borel-semiregular, we simply write f in place of Λf in what follows.
To show Theorem 3.1.12, we need the following lemma.

satisfy (3.1.5). Assume that {S2−k}k∈Z is a homogeneous approximation of the identity of
order ε in Definition 2.3.1 and let Dk := S2−k −S2−k+1 for any k ∈Z. Let g(f) for any
f ∈( ˚Gε

Atomic Characterization
Let (X, q, µ) be an ultra-RD-space with upper dimension n ∈(0, ∞) and diam (X) =
∞. The main target of this section is to establish Theorem 3.2.17 below, namely the
atomic characterization of Hp(X) with p ∈(

parameter q; see Theorem 3.2.9 below. Then we show that ˚Hp,2
at (X) coincides with Hp(X);
see Theorem 3.2.16 below.
To this end, we begin with recalling the notion of atoms as follows.
Definition 3.2.1. Let (X, q, µ) be a quasi-ultrametric space of homogeneous type. Assume
that p ∈(0, 1], q ∈(p, ∞] ∩[1, ∞], and ρ ∈q is such that all ρ-balls are µ-measurable.
A µ-measurable function a : X →C is called a (ρ, p, q)-atom if there exist x ∈X and
r ∈(0, ∞) such that
(i) supp a := {x ∈X : a(x) , 0} ⊂Bρ(x, r).
(ii) ||a||Lq(X) ≤[(Vρ)r(x)]

Remark 3.2.2.
(i) It is easy to see from Definition 3.2.1, (1.1.4), and (1.1.18) that, if
a : X →C is a (ρ, p, q)-atom supported in Bρ(x, r) for some x ∈X and r ∈(0, ∞),
then, for any other ρ′ ∈q with the property that all ρ′-balls are µ-measurable, there
exist positive constants C and c (depending only on ρ, ρ′, and µ) such that Ca is a
(ρ′, p, q)-atom supported in Bρ′(x, cr). As such, we sometimes simply refer to a as
a (p, q)-atom or a (p, q)-atom modulo a harmless constant multiple.
(ii) Note that a (p, q)-atom is in L1(X). Indeed, using H¨older’s inequality and both (i)
and (ii) of Definition 3.2.1, we obtain

have the following conclusion.
Theorem 3.2.9. Let (X, q, µ) be a quasi-ultrametric space of homogeneous type with up-
per dimension n ∈(0, ∞). Let p ∈(0, 1], q ∈(p, ∞] ∩[1, ∞], and ε, β, γ ∈(0, ∞) satisfy

at (X) = ˚Hp,2

at (X) is independent of the choice of q.

□
Remark 3.2.15. In the proof of Theorem 3.2.14, we decompose the elements of Hp(X)
into the linear combination of atoms, while He et al. [102, Proposition 5.7] only de-
composed that into a linear combination of molecules and then used wavelet reproducing
formulae to first obtain the molecular characterization of Hp(X). This is because the ap-
proximation of the identity {S2−k}k∈Z have bounded supports [see Definition 1.2.1(i) above]
in this monograph while that in [102, Definition 2.7] may not have bounded supports.
Using Theorems 3.2.12 and 3.2.14, we obtain the following conclusion.

In this section, we separately characterize Hp(X) by the Littlewood–Paley g-function
and g∗

section are Theorems 3.3.1 and 3.3.6 below.
We first consider the Littlewood–Paley g-function characterization of Hp(X). More
specifically, we have the following conclusion.

(i) Note that we used the same homogeneous approximation of the

0(β, γ))′
with ε, β, and γ as in Theorem 3.3.6.
(ii) In the proof of Theorem 3.3.6, the reverse doubling condition (1.1.22) is needed
since we use Theorem 3.1.6 to guarantee that Hp(X) is independent of the choices
of the homogeneous approximation of the identity and hence Hp(X) defined by
means of different Lusin area functions coincide with each other with equivalent
quasi-norms; see (3.3.17) and (3.3.19). In this case, we also need p >

n+ind (X,q) due

(iii) Let X be a homogeneous group, which is also an ultra-RD-space, with upper dimen-

g∗

Chapter 4
Littlewood–Paley Characterizations of
Triebel–Lizorkin Spaces on
Ultra-RD-Spaces
In this chapter, we establish various Littlewood–Paley function characterizations of the
homogeneous (as well as the inhomogeneous) Triebel–Lizorkin spaces, for the optimal
ranges s ∈(−ind (X, q), ind (X, q)) and
max
(

)
< p, q ≤∞,
on standard ultra-RD-spaces (X, q, µ) with upper dimension n ∈(0, ∞), where µ is as-
sumed to be a Borel-semiregular measure on X. Recall that a quasi-ultrametric space
(X, q) is said to be standard if, for any x ∈X and for some (and hence for all) ρ ∈q,
rρ(x) = 0, where rρ(x) is the same as in (1.1.20). Throughout Sections 4.1–4.4, we always
assume that diam (X) = ∞and hence µ(X) = ∞, which is a quite natural assumption
because of the homogeneity of the homogeneous Triebel–Lizorkin spaces. In Section 4.1,
we recall the definition of homogeneous Triebel–Lizorkin spaces ˙F p,q
s (X). Then we es-
tablish the Lusin area function and the Littlewood–Paley g∗
λ-function characterizations of
˙F p,q
s (X) with p < ∞in Sections 4.2 and 4.3, respectively. The analogous characterizations
of ˙F∞,q
s
(X) are recorded in Section 4.4. We extend all of the corresponding results to the
inhomogeneous case in Sections 4.5 and 4.6, where we have no restriction on diam (X).
4.1
Homogeneous Triebel–Lizorkin Spaces ˙F p,q
s (X)
In this section, we recall the definitions of the homogeneous Triebel–Lizorkin spaces,
the homogeneous Lusin area functions, and the homogeneous Littlewood–Paley func-
tions. We always assume that, for any ρ ∈q, diamρ(X) = ∞and hence µ(X) = ∞.
We now recall the definition of the homogeneous Triebel–Lizorkin spaces on quasi-
ultrametric spaces of homogeneous type; see [7, Definitions 9.2 and 9.6] for standard
Ahlfors-regular quasi-ultrametric spaces with a Borel-semiregular measure, [99, Defini-
tions 5.8(ii), 5.29(ii), 6.7, and 6.14] for standard doubling metric spaces with a Borel
regular measure, and [209, Definitions 3.1(ii), 4.1(ii), 5.1, and 5.9] for standard quasi-
metric spaces of homogeneous type with a Borel regular measure. Recall that, for any
r ∈R, r+ := max{0, r}.
