Remark 4.1.3. The following statements regarding Definition 4.1.1 are true.
(i) If ρ ∈q is an ultrametric, then ind (X, q) = ∞and the smoothness parameter s can
be chosen arbitrarily from (−∞, ∞).
(ii) Let (X, q, µ) be an ultra-RD-space and µ be a Borel-semiregular measure on X. Then
the definition of ˙F p,q
s (X) is independent of the choice of the approximation of the
identity (see Remarks 4.2.3 and 4.4.5 below) as well as the choice of the indices
β, γ; see also [99, Propositions 5.6 and 6.5] and [99, Propositions 5.7 and 6.6],
respectively. Moreover, by Remark 1.2.2(iii), we find that ˙F p,q
s (X) is independent
of the choice of the quasi-ultrametric ρ if ρ ∈q is such that Dk f for any k ∈Z and
f ∈( ˚Gε
0(β, γ))′ is µ-measurable.
(iii) By considering ˙F p,q
s (X) with an equivalence relation, ∼, defined by f ∼g if and
only if f −g is a constant on X, the space ˙F p,q
s (X)/ ∼is quasi-Banach for all s, p, q,
β, and γ as in Definition 4.1.1, and genuinely Banach whenever p, q ≥1; see also
[99, Propositions 5.10(vi) and 6.9(v)].

Next, we prove Theorem 4.2.1.
Proof of Theorem 4.2.1. Let ρ ∈q be a regularized quasi-ultrametric. Note that all of
the conclusions of Theorem 4.2.1 will follow once we establish (4.2.1). To this end,
we first show the second inequality of (4.2.1). On the one hand, by Remark 1.1.44 and

4.3
Littlewood–Paley g∗
λ-Function Characterization of
˙F p,q
s (X)
In this section, we aim to establish the Littlewood–Paley g∗
λ-function characterization of
˙F p,q
s (X) on an ultra-RD-space (X, q, µ). We always assume that, for any ρ ∈q, diamρ(X) =
∞and hence µ(X) = ∞. The main results of this section are Propositions 4.3.1 and 4.3.4
below. We achieve these by using Theorem 4.2.1 and the following arguments. On the
one hand, it is straightforward to show that the Lusin area function is pointwise controlled
by the Littlewood–Paley g∗
λ-function. On the other hand, to estimate the Lp norm of the
Littlewood–Paley g∗
λ-function via that of the Lusin are function, we divide the proof into
two cases, where we use a duality argument and Tonelli’s theorem when p ∈[q, ∞) and
an auxiliary norm estimate related to both the homogeneous Lusin area function and the
homogeneous Littlewood–Paley auxiliary function (see Proposition 4.3.3 below) when
p ∈(0, q).
We first consider the case p ∈[q, ∞).

Remark 4.5.3. The following statements regarding Definition 4.5.1 are true.
(i) If q contains an ultrametric, then ind (X, q) = ∞and s in Definition 4.5.1 can be
chosen arbitrarily from (−∞, ∞).
(ii) Let (X, q, µ) be an ultra-RD-space and µ be a Borel-semiregular measure on X.
Then the definition of F p,q
s (X) is independent of the choice of the approximation of
the identity (see Remarks 4.6.5(i) and 4.6.10(i) below) as well as the choice of the
indices β, γ; see also [99, Propositions 5.27 and 6.17] and [99, Propositions 5.28
and 6.18], respectively. Moreover, by Remark 1.2.2(iii), we find that F p,q
s (X) is
independent of the choice of the quasi-ultrametric ρ if ρ ∈q is such that Dk f for
any k ∈Z+ and f ∈(Gε
0(β, γ))′ is µ-measurable.
(iii) The space F p,q
s (X) is quasi-Banach for all s, p, q, β, and γ as in Definition 4.5.1,
and genuinely Banach whenever p, q ≥1; see also [99, Propositions 5.31(vii)
and 6.21(vi)].

## Page 274


Chapter 5
Real-Variable Theory of
Hardy–Lorentz Spaces on
Ultra-RD-Spaces
The main target of this chapter is to establish various real-variable characterizations of
Hardy–Lorentz spaces Hp,q(X) for the optimal ranges
p ∈
 
n
n + ind (X, q), 1
#
and q ∈(0, ∞]
on ultra-RD-spaces (X, q, µ), where µ is a Borel-semiregular measure and n ∈(0, ∞)
is the upper dimension of (X, q, µ). In this chapter, unless otherwise specified, we al-
low µ(X) to be finite or infinite (equivalently, by Remark 1.1.23, X can be bounded or
unbounded). Specifically, in Section 5.1, we first recall the definition and some basic
properties of Lorentz spaces. Then, by means of different types of maximal functions,
we introduce several brands of Hardy–Lorentz spaces and further show that all these
Hardy–Lorentz spaces are equivalent. In Section 5.2, we establish the atomic and the
finite atomic characterizations of Hardy–Lorentz spaces. In Section 5.3, we establish the
molecular characterization of Hardy–Lorentz spaces. In Section 5.4, we first introduce the
Hardy–Lorentz space by means of the Lusin area function and then prove that it is inde-
pendent of the choice of approximations of the identity. Next, we establish its atomic and
its Littlewood–Paley function characterizations. In Section 5.5, we obtain the interpola-
tion properties of Hardy–Lorentz spaces via the real method. In Section 5.6, we establish
the duality theorem for Hardy–Lorentz spaces. In Section 5.7, we show the boundedness
of Calder´on–Zygmund operators on Hardy–Lorentz spaces. It is important to emphasize
that many of the main results in this chapter are established in the more general setting of
quasi-ultrametric spaces of homogeneous type.
5.1
Maximal Theory of Hardy–Lorentz Spaces
In this section, we first recall the definition of Lorentz spaces and collect some its
properties. Then we introduce several brands of Hardy–Lorentz spaces based on different
maximal functions and prove that all of these spaces coincide.
258


## Page 275


Real-Variable Theory of Hardy–Lorentz Spaces on Ultra-RD-Spaces
259
Let (X, µ) be an arbitrary measure space.
To define Lorentz spaces, we need the
following notion. The non-increasing rearrangement Rf of any µ-measurable function
f : X →R := R ∪{±∞} is defined by setting, for any t ∈R+,
Rf(t) := inf
n
α ∈R+ : df(α) ≤t
o

Here, and thereafter, for any α ∈R+,
df(α) := µ ({x ∈X : |f(x)| > α})
denotes the distribution function of f. Note that we adopt the convention inf ∅= ∞. The
function Rf : R+ →[0, ∞] is right continuous and hence measurable on R+ with respect
to the 1-dimensional Lebesgue measure; see, for instance, [86, Proposition 1.4.5].
In the above context, for any p, q ∈(0, ∞], the Lorentz space Lp,q(X) is defined to be
the set of all µ-measurable functions f : X →R such that
∥f∥Lp,q(X) :=

We take a moment to make a few comments and collect a few basic properties of
Lp,q(X) spaces. The reader is referred to [86, Section 1.4] for a more detailed account of
these spaces. As is the case with the Lebesgue space Lp(X), two functions in Lp,q(X) are
regarded as equal if they coincide pointwise µ-almost everywhere on X. Moreover, the
spaces Lp,q(X) are quasi-Banach spaces with respect to the quasi-norm ∥· ∥Lp,q(X) for all
p, q ∈(0, ∞]. The next proposition highlights the fact that ∥· ∥Lp,q(X) scales naturally under
power dilations; see, for instance, [86, Remark 1.4.7].
Proposition 5.1.1. Let (X, µ) be a measure space, p, q ∈(0, ∞], and r ∈(0, ∞). Then, for
any µ-measurable function f : X →R,
∥|f|r∥Lp,q(X) = ∥f∥r
Lpr,qr(X).
The following proposition is precisely [86, Proposition 1.4.9] and it provides an im-
portant description of the ∥· ∥Lp,q(X) quasi-norm in terms of the distribution function df.
Proposition 5.1.2. Let (X, µ) be a measure space, p ∈(0, ∞), and q ∈(0, ∞]. Then, for
any µ-measurable function f,
∥f∥Lp,q(X) = p

The scale of Lorentz spaces naturally contains Lebesgue spaces and weak Lebesgue
spaces. Indeed, it is easy to see from Proposition 5.1.2 and the definition of ∥· ∥Lp,q(X)
that Lp,p(X) = Lp(X) for all p ∈(0, ∞] and, when q = ∞, a simple power rescaling of

Real-Variable Theory of Hardy–Lorentz Spaces on Ultra-RD-Spaces
t in the definition of ∥· ∥Lp,q(X) gives Lp,∞(X) = weak-Lp(X) for all p ∈(0, ∞). It is also
worth noting that, when p = ∞, L∞,q(X) = {0} for all q ∈(0, ∞); see, for instance, [86,
Example 1.4.8].
As illustrated by the next proposition, the spaces Lp,q(X) are nested in the second ex-
ponent q; see, for instance, [86, Proposition 1.4.10].
Proposition 5.1.3. Let (X, µ) be a measure space, p, q ∈(0, ∞], and s ∈(0, q]. Then
Lp,s(X) ⊂Lp,q(X) and there exists a positive constant Cp,q,s, depending on p, q, and s,
such that, for any µ-measurable function f : X →R,
∥f∥Lp,q(X) ≤Cp,q,s∥f∥Lp,s(X).
Collecting these observations, we have the following embeddings. That is, for any
0 < q ≤p ≤∞,
Lp,q(X) ,→Lp(X) ,→Lp,∞(X).
We conclude our brief survey of Lorentz spaces with the following proposition which
collects a few additional properties of Lp,q(X) spaces; see, for instance, [86, Proposi-
tion 1.4.5 and Example 1.4.8].
Proposition 5.1.4. Let (X, µ) be a measure space and p, q ∈(0, ∞]. Then the following
statements hold.
(i) If f ∈Lp,q(X), then, for µ-almost every x ∈X, |f(x)| < ∞.
(ii) If f, g : X →R are two µ-measurable functions such that |f| ≤|g| pointwise µ-
almost everywhere on X, then ∥f∥Lp,q(X) ≤∥g∥Lp,q(X).
(iii) If p ∈(0, ∞) and E ⊂X is a µ-measurable set, then ∥1E∥Lp,q(X) ∼[µ(E)]
1
p, where the
positive equivalence constants are independent of E.
Moving on, we introduce the following three maximal functions that will in turn be
used to define different brands of maximal Hardy–Lorentz spaces.

The following lemma details the action of the Hardy–Littlewood maximal operator
on Lorentz spaces and it is a slight generalization of [234, Lemma 3.5] wherein Zhou
et al. [234, Lemma 3.5] considered symmetric quasi-metrics in unbounded spaces of
homogeneous type where µ({x}) = 0 for all x ∈X.
Lemma 5.1.13. Let (X, q, µ) be a quasi-ultrametric space of homogeneous type and ρ ∈q
a regularized quasi-ultrametric. Then, for any p ∈(1, ∞] and q ∈(0, ∞],
Mρ : Lp,q(X) →Lp,q(X)
is well defined and bounded.
Proof. Let E ⊂X be an arbitrary µ-measurable set satisfying µ(E) < ∞. By Lemma 1.2.9,
we find that Mρ is bounded on L∞(X) and from L1(X) to L1,∞(X), which implies
Mρ1E
L1,∞(X) ≲∥1E∥L1(X) = µ(E)
and
Mρ1E
L∞,∞(X) =
Mρ1E
L∞(X) ≲∥1E∥L∞(X) = 1.
Therefore, it follows from [138, Theorem 1.1 and Remark 1.4] (used here with p0 = q0 =
1 and p1 = q1 = ∞) that there exists a positive constant C such that, for any f ∈Lp,q(X),
∥Mρ f∥Lp,q(X) ≤C∥f∥Lp,q(X).
This finishes the proof of Lemma 5.1.13.
□

The stage has now been set for us to present the main theorem of this section, which
shows that the three brands of Hardy–Lorentz spaces defined in Definition 5.1.8 coin-
cide with equivalent quasi-norms. The following theorem focuses on the case where
diam (X) = ∞which is equivalent to µ(X) = ∞; see Theorem 5.1.18 below for the case
where diam (X) < ∞.

To show Theorem 5.2.6, we need several technical lemmas. The following lemma is
a Whitney decomposition theorem of open sets proven in [6, Theorem 6.2] (see also [7,
Theorem 2.4]) which is a slight generalization of [168, Theorem 4.21].

Remark 5.2.12. In Lemma 5.2.11, the flexibility in choosing Λ is implicit in the con-
struction in the proofs of [6, Theorem 6.2] and [168, Theorem 4.21].
The following lemma shows the existence of a partition of unity on geometrically dou-
bling quasi-ultrametric spaces, which is exactly [7, Theorem 2.5] and also a direct result
of [6, Theorem 5.1].

Lemma 5.2.15. Let (X, q, µ) be a quasi-ultrametric space of homogeneous type and 0 <
β, γ < ε ⪯ind (X, q). Let {S2−k}k∈Z be a homogeneous approximation of the identity of
order ε as in Definition 2.3.1 and f ∈G(x0, r0, β, γ) for some x0 ∈X and r0 ∈(0, ∞).
Then there exists a positive constant C, independent of x0, r0, and f, such that
(i) For any k ∈Z satisfying r0 ≥2−k,
∥S2−k f∥G(x0,r0,β,γ) ≤C∥f∥G(x0,r0,β,γ).
(ii) For any k ∈Z satisfying r0 < 2−k,
∥S2−k f∥G(x0,2−k,β,γ) ≤C∥f∥G(x0,r0,β,γ).

Definition 5.2.17. Let (X, q, µ) be a quasi-ultrametric space of homogeneous type. As-
sume that p ∈(0, 1], q ∈(p, ∞] ∩[1, ∞], and ρ ∈q is such that all ρ-balls are µ-
measurable. A µ-measurable function a : X →C is called a local (ρ, p, q)-atom if there
exist x ∈X and r ∈(0, ∞) such that
(i) supp a := {x ∈X : a(x) , 0} ⊂Bρ(x, r);

Remark 5.2.18.
(i) It is easy to see from Definition 5.2.17, (1.1.4), and (1.1.18) that,
if a : X →C is a local (ρ, p, q)-atom supported in Bρ(x, r) for some x ∈X and r ∈
(0, ∞), then, for any other ρ′ ∈q with the property that all ρ′-balls are µ-measurable,
there exist positive constants C and c (depending only on ρ, ρ′, and µ) such that Ca
is a local (ρ′, p, q)-atom supported in Bρ′(x, cr). As such, we sometimes simply
refer to a as a (p, q)-atom or a (p, q)-atom modulo a harmless constant multiple.
(ii) Note that a (p, q)-atom is also a local (p, q)-atom. Indeed, when µ(X) = ∞, it holds
automatically. When µ(X) < ∞, we only need to prove that a := [µ(X)]−1

Based on the local atoms in Definition 5.2.17, we introduce the following concept of
local atomic Hardy–Lorentz spaces.
Definition 5.2.19. Let (X, q, µ) be a quasi-ultrametric space of homogeneous type, ρ ∈q
have the property that all ρ-balls are µ-measurable, p ∈(0, 1], q ∈(0, ∞], r ∈(p, ∞] ∩
[1, ∞], and 0 < β, γ < ε < ∞. The local atomic Hardy–Lorentz space hp,q,r
at
(X, ρ) is
defined to be the same as in Definition 5.2.1 but only with (p, r)-atoms therein replaced
by local (p, r)-atoms.
To show the equivalence between Hp,q,r
at
(X, ρ) and hp,q,r
at
(X, ρ), we need the following
proposition.

5.2.2
Finite Atomic characterization
In this subsection, we establish the finite atomic characterization of Hp,q
∗(X). To be pre-
cise, we prove that, for any given finite linear combination of (p, r)-atoms [or continuous
(p, ∞)-atoms], its Hp,q
∗(X) (quasi-)norm can be achieved via all its finite atomic decompo-
sitions (see Theorem 5.2.31). When establishing the boundedness of linear operators on
Hardy spaces, one typically resorts to its (infinite) atomic characterization since estimates
on the linear operator can, in principle, be reduced to studying the action of the operator
on individual atoms. However, in light of an example presented in [23] (which is based
on a result due to Y. Meyer in [167]) one must exercise great care when interchanging a
linear operator and an infinite linear combination of atoms. The advantage of these finite
decompositions is that one does not need to be concerned about the convergence of the
summation. This perspective will be useful in Section 5.7.
We begin with the following definition of finite atomic Hardy–Lorentz spaces.

Definition 5.2.25. Let (X, q) be a quasi-ultrametric space and ρ ∈q.
The space of
uniformly continuous functions UC(X, ρ) on X is defined to be the set of all functions
f : X →C satisfying that, for any given η ∈(0, ∞), there exists δ ∈(0, ∞) such that, for
any x, y ∈X with ρ(x, y) < δ,
|f(x) −f(y)| < η.
Remark 5.2.26. In the context of Definition 5.2.25, it is easy to see that, for any β ∈
(0, ∞),
˙Cβ(X) ⊂UC(X, ρ)
and, for any ρ′ ∈q,
UC(X, ρ) = UC(X, ρ′).
Thus, we sometimes simply write UC(X) instead of UC(X, ρ).

5.3
Molecular Characterization
In this section, we establish the molecular characterization of the maximal-function-
based Hardy space Hp,q
∗(X) for the optimal ranges
p ∈
 
n
n + ind (X, q), 1
#
and q ∈(0, ∞]
on ultra-RD spaces with upper dimension n ∈(0, ∞). Indeed, the molecular characteriza-
tion established in this section also holds in the more general setting of quasi-ultrametric
spaces of homogeneous type for the same range of p and q above, but with a different
range of parameters for the molecular Hardy–Lorentz space [see Remark 5.3.7(i)]. As we
see in Section 5.7, the importance of such a characterization naturally arises when one is
seeking to extend a bounded linear operator on L2(X) to a bounded operator on Hp,q
∗(X).
We begin with the following notion of (ρ, p, r, η)-molecules.
Definition 5.3.1. Let (X, q, µ) be a quasi-ultrametric space of homogeneous type, p ∈
(0, 1], r ∈(p, ∞] ∩[1, ∞], η ∈(0, ∞), and ρ ∈q have the property that all ρ-balls are
µ-measurable. A function m ∈Lr(X) is called a (ρ, p, r, η)-molecule if there exist x ∈X
and r ∈(0, ∞) such that
(i) ∥m1Bρ(x,r)∥Lr(X) ≤[(Vρ)r(x)]

(i) It is easy to see from Definition 5.3.1, (1.1.4), and (1.1.18) that,

and η) such that Cm is a (ρ′, p, r, η)-molecule centered at Bρ′(x, cr). As such, we
sometimes simply refer to m as a (p, r, η)-molecule or a (p, r, η)-molecule modulo a
harmless constant multiple.

5.4
Littlewood–Paley Function Characterizations
In Chapter 3, we introduced the Hardy space Hp(X) defined via the Lusin area func-
tion and established its atomic and Littlewood–Paley function characterizations. In this
section, we generalize these results by establishing the atomic and the Littlewood–paley
function characterizations of Hardy–Lorentz spaces on ultra-RD-spaces, (X, q, µ), for the
optimal ranges
p ∈
 
n
n + ind (X, q), 1
#
and q ∈(0, ∞].
In contrast to Sections 5.1–5.3, we use ( ˚Gε
0(β, γ))′ as the considered space of distributions
(see Definition 2.1.12 for the definition of this space). Throughout this section, we assume
that, for some (hence all) ρ ∈q, diamρ(X) = ∞, which, by Remark 1.1.23, is equivalent
to the demand that µ(X) = ∞.

5.4.1
Hardy–Lorentz Spaces via Lusin Area Functions
Let (X, q, µ) be a quasi-ultrametric space of homogeneous type and 0 < β, γ < ε ⪯
ind (X, q). Assume that {S2−k}k∈Z is a homogeneous approximation of the identity of order
ε as in Definition 2.3.1 and, for any k ∈Z, Dk := S2−k −S2−k+1. For any f ∈( ˚Gε
0(β, γ))′, let
the Lusin area function S θ(f) with aperture θ ∈(0, ∞), the Littlewood–Paley g-function
g( f), and the Littlewood–Paley g∗
λ-function g∗
λ(f) with λ ∈(0, ∞) of f be the same as,
respectively, in (3.1.1), (3.1.2), and (3.1.3). As before, we denote S 1 simply by S . Recall
from Remark 3.1.2 that we may assume S θ(f), g(f), and g∗
λ(f) are constructed using a
regularized quasi-ultrametric belonging to q as in Definition 1.1.16. In particular, the use
of a regularized quasi-ultrametric guarantees that S θ(f), g(f), and g∗
λ(f) are µ-measurable;
see Lemma 3.1.3.
Now, we first introduce the following concept of Lusin-area-function-based Hardy–
Lorentz spaces.
Definition 5.4.1. Let (X, q, µ) be a quasi-ultrametric space of homogeneous type with
upper dimension n ∈(0, ∞), p ∈(0, ∞), q ∈(0, ∞], and n( 1
p −1)+ < β, γ < ε ⪯ind (X, q).
The Lusin-area-function-based Hardy–Lorentz space Hp,q(X) is defined by setting
Hp,q(X) :=
n

Remark 5.4.2. Let the notation be as in Definition 5.4.1.
(i) Let p ∈(
n
n+ind (X,q), ∞). Then Hp,q(X) is a quasi-Banach space, which can be seen
from Theorem 5.4.11 and Remark 5.4.7(ii) below.
(ii) Recall that, for any p ∈(0, ∞], Lp,p(X) = Lp(X), which implies that
Hp,p(X) = Hp(X) :=
n
f ∈( ˚Gε
0(β, γ))′ : ∥f∥Hp(X) := ∥S (f)∥Lp(X) < ∞

(iii) From the assumption that n( 1

infer that, for any p ∈(0,
n

only contains constant functions.
Next, we prove that Hp,q(X) defined in Definition 5.4.1 is independent of the choice
of homogeneous approximations of the identity {S2−k}k∈Z in Definition 2.3.1. The main
ingredient in this vein is the following lemma which is a generalization of the Fefferman–
Stein vector-valued inequality in Lemma 3.1.11 from Lebesgue spaces to Lorentz spaces.
This result extends [234, Theorem 7.3] from nonatomic spaces of homogeneous type to
quasi-ultrametric spaces of homogeneous type.
Lemma 5.4.3. Let (X, q, µ) be a quasi-ultrametric space of homogeneous type and assume

Remark 5.4.13. Zhou et al. [234, Theorem 7.10] established the atomic characteriza-
tion of the Lusin-area-function-based Hardy–Lorentz space Hp,q(X) by decomposing the
elements of Hp,q(X) into the linear combination of molecules and then using a wavelet
reproducing formula to first obtain its molecule characterization. In contrast, in the proof
of Theorem 5.4.11, we are able to directly decompose the elements of Hp,q(X) into linear
combination of atoms since our approximation of the identity has bounded support (see
Definition 1.2.1(i)) while the approximation of the identity in [234, Definition 2.8] only
has exponential decay.
5.4.3
Littlewood–Paley Function Characterizations
In this subsection, we establish the Littlewood–Paley g-function and the Littlewood–
Paley g∗
λ-function characterizations of Hp,q(X), where Hp,q(X) is the Lusin-area-function-
based Hardy–Lorentz space in Definition 5.4.1. The reader is reminded that g and g∗
λ were
defined, respectively, in (3.1.2) and (3.1.3).

Theorem 3.3.1 from Hardy spaces to Hardy–Lorentz spaces.

5.5
Real Interpolation of Hardy–Lorentz Spaces
In this section, we consider the interpolation properties of Hardy–Lorentz spaces via
the real method on general quasi-ultrametric spaces of homogeneous type. We first recall
some basic notions about the real interpolation. A pair of quasi-normed spaces (X1, X2)
is called a compatible couple of quasi-normed spaces if X1 and X2 are two quasi-normed
linear spaces which are continuously embedded in some larger topological vector space.
Let
X1 + X2 := { f1 + f2 : f1 ∈X1, f2 ∈X2} .
The Peetre K-functional on (X1, X2) is defined by setting, for any t ∈(0, ∞] and f ∈
X1 + X2,
K(t, f; X1, X2) := inf ∥f1∥X1 + t∥f2∥X2 : f = f1 + f2, f1 ∈X1, f2 ∈X2
	 .
For any θ ∈(0, 1) and q ∈(0, ∞], the interpolation space (X1, X2)θ,q is defined by setting

Remark 5.7.2.
(i) Let η ∈(0, ∞) and ⃗η := {2−kη}k∈N. Then the (ρ, p, r, −→η )-molecule in
Definition 5.7.1 reduces to the (ρ, p, r, η)-molecule in Definition 5.3.1.
(ii) It is easy to see from Definition 5.7.1, (1.1.4), and (1.1.18) that, if m : X →C is a
(ρ, p, r,⃗η)-molecule centered at Bρ(x, r) for some x ∈X and r ∈(0, ∞), then, for any
other ρ′ ∈q with the property that all ρ′-balls are µ-measurable, there exist positive
constants C and c (depending only on ρ, ρ′, µ, and ⃗η) such that Cm is a (ρ′, p, r,⃗η)-
molecule centered at Bρ′(x, cr). As such, we sometimes simply refer to m as a
(p, r,⃗η)-molecule or a (p, r,⃗η)-molecule modulo a harmless constant multiple.
(iii) Conditions (i) and (ii) in Definition 5.7.1 and (5.7.1) ensure that any (ρ, p, r,⃗η)-

The following lemma is a generalization of [234, Lemma 8.15] by allowing the measure
of a singleton to be strictly positive and ρ ∈q to be a quasi-ultrametric. Note that, when
ρ ∈q is a regularized quasi-ultrametric, we have, for any given x0 ∈X, the mapping
x 7→Vρ(x0, x) is µ-measurable on X.

Remark 5.7.10. Let (X, ρ, µ) be a space of homogeneous type with upper dimension
n ∈(0, ∞), and assume that ε ∈(0, η), where η ∈(0, 1) is the smoothness index of the
wavelets on X constructed by Auscher and Hyt¨onen in [16], and that T is an ε-Calder´on–
Zygmund operator as in Definition 2.2.3. Zhou et al. [234, Theorems 8.12 and 8.13] and

## Page 429


Abstract
Let (X, q, µ) be an ultra-RD-space with upper dimension n ∈(0, ∞); i.e., it is a quasi-
ultrametric space of homogeneous type whose measure µ satisfies an additional reverse
doubling property. Let ind (X, q) ∈(0, ∞] denote its lower smoothness index, as intro-
duced by Mitrea et al. In this monograph, the authors first construct a new approximation
of the identity on quasi-ultrametric spaces of homogeneous type, achieving a maximal
degree of smoothness 0 < ε ⪯ind (X, q). This fundamental tool is then used to de-
rive sharp homogeneous (as well as inhomogeneous) continuous/discrete Calder´on repro-
ducing formulae on ultra-RD-spaces. As applications, the authors establish Littlewood–
Paley function characterizations for both Hardy spaces and Triebel–Lizorkin spaces on
ultra-RD-spaces. The authors further introduce Hardy–Lorentz spaces Hp,q
∗(X) via the
grand maximal function, with the sharp range p ∈(
n
n+ind (X,q), ∞) and q ∈(0, ∞], and
provide their real-variable characterizations using radial/non-tangential maximal func-
tions, (finite) atoms, molecules, and various Littlewood–Paley functions. Based on these
characterizations, the authors prove a duality theorem between Hardy–Lorentz spaces
and Campanato–Lorentz spaces, establish a real interpolation theorem for Hardy–Lorentz
spaces, and derive boundedness results for Calder´on–Zygmund operators on them. It
should be emphasized that many of the main results in this monograph are indeed estab-
lished in the more general setting of quasi-ultrametric spaces of homogeneous type.
The novelty of this monograph resides in two key aspects. First, the assumptions on
the underlying space are minimal: the authors only assume that µ is Borel-semiregular,
that singletons may have strictly positive measure, and that ρ is a quasi-ultrametric, which
provides a more general theoretic setting than the Coifman and Weiss notion of spaces of
homogeneous type. Second, the maximal order of smoothness achieved by the approxi-
mation of the identity and the regularity exponent in the Calder´on reproducing formulae
are both sharp, as they are intrinsically based on the sharp regularity index ind (X, q) of
the quasi-ultrametric. Given the sharpness of these tools, the authors are able to develop
the real-variable theory of the Hardy–Lorentz space Hp,q
∗(X) for optimal ranges of p and q
(which cannot be obtained in any of the related results currently available in the literature),
under minimal assumptions on X.
All results presented here are new and to appear in print for the first time. Designed to
be largely self-contained, this monograph is an invaluable resource for graduate students
and researchers interested in the frontier of the function space theory on ultra-RD spaces.
413
